## Page 1


Skands and coskands
(The non-founded set theory with
individuals and its model in the Field of
all Conway numbers)
Ju. T. Lisica∗
St. Tikhon’s Orthodox Humanities University
Likhov Lane 6, p. 1, Moscow, 127051,
e-mail: jutlisica@yandex.ru
Abstract
The basic one in this work is the axiomatic set theory NBG (von
Neumann-Bernays-G¨odel), which is a first-order theory with its own ax-
ioms, including in particular the axiom of choice AC and the axiom of
regularity RA. The universal class V of all sets in this theory exactly
coincides with the class of all founded sets, i.e., such X ∈V that does not
exist an infinitely descending ∈-sequence X ∋X1 ∋X2 ∋... ∋Xn ∋... of
sets Xn, n = 1, 2, 3, ... .
In the first part of the paper, a new concept of skand is introduced
– a random aggregate, or “decreasing“ tuple composed of founded sets,
e.g., X = {1, {2, {3, {... ...}}}}, and the theory of NBG−= NBG −RA,
i.e., the theory of NBG without the axiom of regularity RA, to which
is added the new axiom SEA of the existence of infinite-length skands
and the pseudo-founding axiom PFA. These new axioms are a negation
of the axiom of regularity and are thus less restrictive than the axiom of
regularity RA in the sense that they admit the existence of non-founded
sets, and the axiom of regularity excludes the existence of such sets. At
the same time of course the axiom of extensionality EA is replaced by a
more accurate axiom of extensionality EEA, since it takes into account
the equality of new objects.
In the second part of the paper, a new concept of coskand is intro-
duced, which is dual to a notion of skand and is a random aggregate, or
“increasing“ tuple composed of founded sets and the theory of NBG and
actually is a theory NBG[U] with individuals as limiting coskands, e.g.,
X = ...{3, {2, {1, {0}}}}... .
∗Dedicated to my son Andrew Yu.

“compressions“.

## Page 2


New mathematical objects as skands and coskands provide an oppor-
tunity to build more complex formal theories of non-founded sets with
usual individuals and in addition with individuals like coskands as well as
with coskands based over non-founded sets, not only over founded sets.
As a consequence of this theory is the following preferred formulas for
contradiction and existence or nonexistence:
(∃x)(∀y[y ∈x ⇔y /∈y]) ⇒(¬∃x)(∀y[y ∈x ⇔y /∈y]) vs x ∈x ⇔x /∈x
and
¬∃X∀x[x ∈X] vs ∃X¬∃{X}.
This new approach makes it possible to expand the understanding of
the theory of non-founded sets with individuals but also to enrich the
theory with new mathematical objects that were previously considered
undefinable notions like “trans-definite sets“ (see [5], p. 311) and in par-
ticular to bypass paradoxical Russell-type sets, which as a result leads
to new consistent non-mathematical objects, previously conventionally
called sets without boundaries, which go beyond not only the universal
of the proper class V, but in mathematics itself, but having their own
interesting and diverse, say, “geography“.
Models of all these formal set theories are constructed in the Field of
all Conway numbers No. A digest of the main constructions of Conway
number theory and the latest new results of this theory is given.
The difference between infinities (say, +∞ω) and infinite numbers (say,
ω) in the structure of the Conway numbers fields is explained.
The paper also considers previously unnoticed patterns of the infinite
sequences (α)0<α<λ of ordinal numbers 0 < α < λ (λ is a limit ordinal)
and their reciprocals ( 1
α)0<α<λ as “pulsars“ or pulsating “jumps“ and
their corresponding periodic “compressions“, as well as various algebraic
structures of infinitesimal Conway numbers.
Examples of exponential functions y = ωx and y = ex, x ∈No, are
given, for the first of which the argument coincides with the value of the
function at some points, i.e., ωx0 = x0, and for the second, the argument
can be greater than the value of the function, i.e., ex1 < x1, a kind of
anti-Zeno paradox: the tortoise (input) catches up with Achilles (output)
in the first case and the tortoise overtakes him in the second case.
1. Introduction
Axiomatic set theories, more precisely, formal logistic systems that are first-
order theories with logical axioms and proper axioms of set theories (which,
together with the definition of equality of sets, become first-order theories with
equality) are well known and carefully developed. Such, for example, are set
theory ZF with an infinite scheme (system) of Zermelo-Frenkel axioms and set
theory NBG with a finite system of von Neumann-Bernays-G¨odel axioms. Both
of these systems can include the axiom of choice CA and the axiom of regularity
RA. Here we will assume that they are included in the system of axioms of
these theories, and through ZF −and NBG−denote them without the axiom
2


## Page 3


of regularity RA, respectively. The latter, with the existing axiom of choice, is
equivalent to the axiom of foundation FA (see: Proposition 4.39 in [28]).
Therefore, we will talk here about the axiom FA, which says that for any
set X ∈V from the universe V of all sets an infinitely descending ∈-sequence
X ∋X1 ∋X2 ∋... of sets Xn ∈V, n = 1, 2, 3, ... does not exist.
Note also that working with the formal theory of NBG−, some authors (see:
[6], [17]) in those places where one has to use the axiom of choice CA (for any
set of X there is such a choice function F : 2X \∅→X, that F(Y ) ∈Y ) for any
element Y ∈2X \∅), use the Neumann axiom N, which states that the universe
V of all sets of the theory is bijective to the class On of all ordinals, that in
the absence of the axiom of foundation, FA is a stronger axiom than even the
axiom of global choice E (there is a unitary operator σ that maps any nonempty
set X to its element σ(X) ∈X, see: [6], [15]). In this work, we will not need
such a strengthening of the axiom of choice, with the exception of one mention
of the strong axiom of plenitude of individuals SAP, but it is not excluded in
the further development of the theory of non-founded sets.
The axiom of the foundation FA is very important, for example, in the axiom
of the pair PA: for any two different sets X and Y , there is a single set {X, Y },
the elements of which are exactly X and Y and only them. At the same time,
without the foundation axiom, it is impossible to prove that the set {X, Y }
is different from X or from Y , since this, generally speaking, is incorrect: in
non-founded set theories ZF −or NBG−, i.e. without the foundation axiom,
there are such different sets of X and Y that {X, Y } = X or {X, Y } = Y ; for
example, if X = {0} = {{}}
def
= 1 and Y = {1, Y }, then {X, Y } = {1, Y } = Y .
(We will see below that such a set exists.)
On the contrary, in ZF and
NBG it is always {X, Y } ̸= X and {X, Y } ̸= Y , since the disordered pair
{X, Y } of the founded sets X and Y is always founded and therefore is not a
proper element, this means that it does not coincide with either X or Y , since
any family of founded sets always does not coincide with any of its elements
(see: [18], (3.7), p. 94).
This directly follows from the fact that for two founded sets for two founded
sets X and Y the ordered pair Z =< X, Y >
def
= {{X}, {X, Y }} is also a founded
set and Z ̸= X, Z ̸= Y , Z /∈X and Z /∈Y (see: [3], p. 25). For non-founded
sets, this is generally not the case, for example, if X = {X} = {{...}}, and Y =
{X, Y } = {X, {X, Y }}, then Z =< X, Y >= {{X}, {X, Y }} = {X, {X, Y }} =
Y and Z ∈Y .
Despite this advantage of accuracy and distinctness in the axiom of a pair
and the definition of an ordered pair, simplifying the definitions of ordinal and
cardinal numbers, ([13], pp. 110, 129 and p. 123) and, for example, that the
generalized continuum hypothesis in this form: 2ℵα = ℵα+1 for any ordinal α
– entails the axiom of choice (without the axiom of regularity, this is not the
case [18], p. 103) and many other advantages, the axiom of regularity (and
with the axiom of choice CA the axiom of foundation FA) in a sense greatly
narrows not only the “class“ of all sets “of naive“ Cantor’s set theory (Cantor’s
“inconsistent multiplicity“), which has no denotation, but also the class V−of
3


## Page 4


all sets of the theory of NBG−.
In this case, the so-called reflexive sets with respect to the membership
relation ∈are excluded, i.e., when the set X is a proper element: X ∈X
(note that the only predicative letter A2
2 of similar formal set theories, which is
usually abbreviated to the symbol of the membership relation ∈, when writing
X ∈Y for A2
2(X, Y ) and X /∈Y for ¬A2
2(X, Y ), does not exclude the reflexive
relation A2
2(X, X), i.e. X ∈X), and the so-called extraordinary Mirimanoff sets
[29] are also excluded, i.e., sets X that have infinitely descending ∈-sequences
X ∋X1 ∋X2 ∋... ∋Xn ∋... . Note that the axiom of foundation is not
restrictive, since the multiplicity of all founded sets in the naive Cantor theory
also remains inconsistent, and in the formal theory NBG is its proper class,
so it is more correct to say that this axiom is not restrictive, but narrows the
field of reasoning of any set theory, although, as in this work It is the basis for
further extensions of the universal class of all sets of formal theories excluding
the axiom of foundation.
The so-called Russell “principle of the vicious circle“ was also excluded.
However, von Neumann [33], introducing the axiom of regularity RA in 1925,
pursued exactly this goal: to prohibit such sets, since it was believed that they
lead to antinomies and paradoxes.
But it immediately became clear that mathematics was deprived of the field
of modeling such natural processes as cyclicity, the study of orthodox statements
such as “statements of a liar“ and many problems of linguistics, later computer
science, graph theory, game theory, flow theory, etc., in which cyclic models lie
outside the universe of founded sets.
The uncertainty and ambiguity of the relation X ∈X was ignored, since the
set X is not defined by its elements in X \ {X}, i.e., by all elements other than
X: with the same elements, there is its proper class of different sets containing
themselves as therefore, the equality X = Y of such sets coincides with the
tautology X = Y ⇔X = Y , therefore, X is not defined as a set, since according
to Cantor: “A set is a collection of M defined and distinguishable objects m“
[10]; and X in a reflexive set, its element equal to the set itself is undefinable
and indistinguishable, therefore, the set itself is undefinable.
A similar uncertainty and ambiguity arises for the Mirimanoff sets X ∋X1 ∋
X2 ∋... in particular for X ∋X ∋X ∋... the irregular set X = {{{...}}}, the
only element of which is the set X itself: there are a lot of such different sets -
they make up their proper class, if one does not introduce an additional axiom
of uniqueness that would identify all the elements of this proper class, see: [1].
Already in the first half of the 20th century, many authors offered their own
“anti-founded axioms“ AFA and simply added their AFA-universes of sets
designed to solve these problems, for example, [16], [6], [7], [36], [17] , etc. All
these theories are consistent if ZF −or NBG−are consistent. Nevertheless, they
themselves differ in the definition of equality in the axiom of extensionality.
In this work, we will construct a new natural theory of unfounded sets that
is different from the ones mentioned above. In some sense, it will be the most
minimal and very similar to the theory of founded sets, in particular, the class
4


## Page 5


of all its sets contains the class of all founded sets as a subclass, and the sets
that are not contained in this class are non-founded but can be said to be
“pseudo-founded“.
Here, for certainty, we will choose the formal theory of NBG as the basis for
constructing our theory of non-founded sets (it would be easy to base the theory
of ZF in the same way) and abandon the axiom of foundation FA, i.e. consider
the theory of NBG−= NBG −FA.
Next, we define a new mathematical
object, which we will call a skand: a random aggregate (or tuple) of founded
sets or their absence. Two new axioms about the existence of skands ESA and
pseudo-founded PFA are restrictive axioms, but less rigid than the axiom of
foundation. Thus, the resulting theory is such an extension of NBG−that it
naturally expands the class V
def
= V(0) of all the founded sets of the theory of
NBG underlying the new theory to the class V(1) ⊂V−, with V(0) ⊂V1.
This is the first step of inductive theory building NBG(ν) when ν = 1.
A similar sequential extension is constructed in the same natural way for
any ordinal number ν > 1.
The first application of the theory of skands is the natural definition of
coskands, which, in the case of their infinite length, are either sets of a new
theory, which is an extension of the theory of NBG−, or new individuals of this
set theory.
Another application of this non-founded set theory is new proofs of the results
of set theory used by “paradoxical sets“, such as the diagonal Cantor set K
def
=
{x /∈φ(x)}, where φ : X →2X the assumed bijection; as the Russell set
R
def
= {X ∈R ⇔X /∈X}; as the Mirimanoff set W of all founded sets; as
a Zermelo set M containing as its elements all its subsets; as a Hilbert set H
constructed naturally from natural numbers. All the results in the theory of
NBG−obtained with the help of these paradoxical sets – that they have no
correspondence under the assumed bijection, or that they are proper classes,
or that they do not exist – are bypassed by the corresponding “paradoxes“.
Moreover, the same method proves that the set S
def
= {X ∈S ⇔X ∈X}
of all reflexive sets is not a set, but is its proper class, although the formal
substitution instead of The X of the assumed set S, which allows the first-order
theory, does not lead to a contradictory formula, as in the Russell paradox.
Thus, the supposed dual “sets“ R and S in set theory become equal and are
their proper classes.
Models for the theory of skands and the theory of coskands have been con-
structed in the universal class No of all Conway numbers. This universal class
has the algebraic structure of a linearly ordered field and can therefore be useful
for models and even non-standard models of other formal theories. The paper
also considers previously unnoticed patterns of the infinite sequences (α)0<α<λ
of ordinal numbers 0 < α < λ (λ is a limit ordinal) and their reciprocals

α)0<α<λ as “pulsars“ or pulsating “jumps“ and their corresponding periodic
“compressions“, as well as various algebraic structures of infinitesimal Conway
numbers.

2. Necessary information from the theory of sets with individuals,
prerequisites and notation
As already mentioned, for the sake of certainty, we will consider here the set
theory NBG, which contains the axiom of choice CA and the axiom of founda-
tion FA, which is equivalent to the axiom of regularity RA. This theory will be
the basis of all our constructions. Since further constructions will use the theory
of sets with individuals, then for the uniformity of the description we consider
the theory of sets with some fixed set or proper class of U individuals (they also
say atoms or proto-elements), which, generally speaking, can be empty (the the-
ory of “pure sets“, considered above in Annotations and in the Introduction).
Let’s denote this theory by NBG[U]. We borrow the information we need here
from the monograph [28], pp. 297-304. Similarly, we can consider the theory of
Zermelo-Frenkel sets with individuals ZF[U].)
1. Set theory NBG−[U] is a formal first-order logical theory with equality,
which is constructed as follows: the variables of this theory are lowercase bold
Latin letters x, y, z,...; there is one binary predicative letter A2
2 and one unary
predicative letter A1
1, which are abbreviated: A2
2(x, y) to x ∈y, ¬A2
2(x, y) to
x /∈y and A1
1(x) to Cls(x), and reads like “x there is a class“. The usual
entries of formulas in “pure“ set theory NBG−, as (∀X)B(X) and (∃X)B(X)
in NBG−[U], written here as (∀x)(Cls(x) ⇒B(x)) and (∃x)(Cls(x) ∧B(x))
accordingly (B(X) means that X has the property B); notation Pr(x) is given
by the formula Cls(x)∧¬M(x), and reads “x has its proper class“; designation
Ur(x) is given by the formula ¬Cls(x), and reads “x there is an individual“.
Thus, in the theory of NBG−[U], two concepts are distinguished: classes
and individuals, as objects of the theory, and classes have two types: sets and
proper classes. Denote by El(x) formula M(x) ∨Ur(x) and reads “x there is
an element“. In this interpretation, sets and individuals are objects that are
elements, or members of classes; proper classes are also objects, but are not
elements of any other objects – sets or proper classes.
The definition of the equality of two theoretical objects NBG−[U], i.e. x = y
is an abbreviation of the following formula:
[Cls(x)∧Cls(y)∧(∀z)(z ∈x ⇔z ∈y)]∨[Ur(x)∧Ur(y)∧(∀z)(x ∈z ⇒y ∈z)].

which we will denote by NBG[U].
Both formal theories are formal theories
(systems) of the first order with equality.
In simple words, in the theory of NBG[U], there are three types of objects:
individuals, sets, and proper classes. Individuals do not contain any objects
of this theory, but they themselves can be contained both in non-empty sets
and in their proper classes; non-empty sets can contain individuals or other
sets, including the empty set ∅, but do not contain their proper classes as their
elements, on the other hand, any set can be contained in another a nonempty set
or a proper class; proper classes contain sets or individuals, but are not contained
either in sets or in proper classes. Moreover, all its objects are founded, i.e. in
one of its objects x the maximum descending ∈sequences x = x1 ∋x2 ∋... ∋xn
8

(Note that the empty set ∅is the only special individual that is conveniently
called a set and is given by the following axiom: (∃x)(M(x))(∀y)(y /∈x); differs
from other individuals in the definition of equality.)

the class of sets bijective to the set X. The possibility of one set X is less
than the cardinality of another set |Y |, i.e. |X| < |Y | if there is a bijection of
the set X onto some subset Y ′ of the set Y . If Y ′ = Y , then |X| = |Y |; this
happens, according to the Cantor-Bernstein theorem, if and only if |X| < |Y |
and |Y | < |X|. If there is no axiom of choice, then two sets X and Y can be
incomparable in power, for example, if the set X is a Dedekind set, i.e. an
infinite set that is not bijective to its proper subset, and Y is any countable set
or any well-ordered set.
For simplicity (abbreviations of writing formulas) we will now denote sets or
classes with capital letters X, omitting predicative letters, and the elements of
these sets or classes x ∈X will be denoted with lowercase letters, and not as
above in bold, also omitting predicative letters.
Using P, we denote the operator that maps to each set X of the theory
NBG[U] the set P(X) of this theory, given by the formula ∀x(x ∈P(X) ≡x ⊆

For technical purposes, we will need the following convention.
Every
set X (or function represented by its graph, or the skand introduced below,
as well as other similar objects of set theory) can be treated as a new indi-
vidual. To do this, we need to apply to X “the forgetful functor“ F, i.e., to
forget the structure in X defined by the membership relation ∈in the object
FX. In particular FX does not contain any elements, i.e., it is an individual.
For example, the natural numbers 0, 1, 2, 3, ... can be considered as individuals
by applying the oblivion functor F to the sets representing them: {}, {{}},
{{}, {{}}}, {{}, {{}}, {{}, {{}}}},... or {}, {0}, {0, 1}, {0, 1, 2}, .... Note that in
this example, the empty set ∅= {} representing the number/individual 0 is itself
an individual, which is usually called a set for convenience of terminology. The
other individuals are not called sets. (It is common to write 0 = {}, 1 = {0},
etc. In our case, it would have to be written as 0 = F{}, 1 = F{0} = F{{}},
etc., but there’s no confusion in the first entry either, so to speak: “unit 1 is an
individual“ and “{0}“ is a set consisting of one element – individual 0).
If we omit the forgetful functor F (more precisely, we apply the composition
of the functors F −1 ◦F, counting the inverse functor F −1 to the functor F by
10


## Page 11


the operator “of remembering“ R = F −1), then from the individual FX we get
the original set R ◦FX = F −1 ◦FX = 1 ◦X = X with all the membership
relations of its elements, i.e. (∀x)x ∈X. Similarly with functions and skands:
after lowering the forgetful functor, the structure of the ∈membership relations
in these objects is restored. □

John Conway in [14] (1979) presented a new and the best construction of a
proper class of numbers which contains all real numbers and all ordinal numbers
as well as many other great and small numbers like magnitudes ω −1, ω

1. Conway construction of numbers was a modification of two well-known
ideas. One of them was the Mirimanoff’s representation [29] (1917), later in 1923
repeated by von Neumann [34], of the ordinal numbers: 0 = {}, 1 = {{}} = {0},
2 = {{}, {{}}} = {0, {0}}, 3 = {{}, {{}}, {{}, {{}}}} = {0, 1, {0, 1}} = {0, 1, 2},
..., ω = {0, 1, 2, ..., n, ...}, ω + 1 = {0, 1, 2, ..., n, ..., ω}, ... and so on.
Another one was the construction of real numbers R [30] (1888) via Dedekind
sections {A | B} of rational numbers Q, i.e., ξ = {A | B} ∈R if and only if A
and B are non-empty sets of rationals such that Q = A ∪B, A ∩B = ∅, A has
no greatest number, and for every a ∈A and b ∈B one has a < b.
Conway definition of a number is the following.
If L and R are any two sets of numbers, and no member of L is ≥any
member of R, then there is a number {L | R}.
If x = {L | R} is a number, then, for short, x = {xL | xR}, where xL is a
typical member of L and xR is a typical member of R, i.e., L = {xL} and
R = {xR} are sets.
Further,
x ≥y iff (no xR ≤y and x ≤no yL);
x ≤y iff y ≥x; as well as
x = y iff (x ≥y and y ≥x);
x > y iff (x ≥y and y ̸≥x);
x < y iff y > x.
Different representations {L′ | R′} and {L | R} can define the same number;
that is why one must distinguish between the form {L | R} of a number and the
number itself. Conway called L the lower class and R the upper class of number
x = {L | R}.
Operations +, · and / on all Conway numbers x, y ∈No are the following:
x + y = {xL + y, x + yL | xR + y, x + yR},

More precisely, for each ordinal number α ∈On Conway defined a set Mα
of numbers by setting x = {xL | xR} in Mα if all the xL and xR are in the union
of all the Mβ for β < α. Then he putted Oα = S

Then in the terminology of numbers’ birth to which he adhered:
Mα is the set of numbers born on or before α (Made numbers),
Nα is the set of numbers born first on day α (New numbers), and
Oα is the set of numbers born before day α (Old numbers).
Each x ∈Nα defines a Dedekind section {L|R} of Oα, if one sets L =
{y ∈Oα : y < x} and R = {y ∈Oα : y > x}, then x = {L | R}. Moreover,
Mα = Oα ∪Nα as the union of Oα together with all its sections, in the natural
order. Notice also that for each number x ∈Mα there are inequalities inequality

{Oα | }, respectively, which dropped the Dedekind restriction on sets L and R
to be nonempty.
Every number x is in a unique set Nα (see [14] p. 30). Taking into account
the above description, we call a birthday form {xL | xR} of x when the birthdays
of all xL, xR are less than α.

Part One
1. Definition of skands
Definition 1. A skand with a clutch region [α0, α) is called an object X,
which for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α) uniquely associates the set X(α′) ∈V[U]
and has the following internal structure of the membership relations ∈of its
constituents and parts: for any ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α) and for any element x ∈
X(α′) there is a relation x ∈X|[α′,α); and for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α), such
that α′ + 1 ∈[α0, α), there is a relation X|[α′+1,α) ∈X|[α′,α), where X|[α′,α)
and X|[α′+1,α) are restrictions of X on [α′, α) and [α′ + 1, α), respectively.
Here for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α) via X|[α′,α) we denote the skand, which is
a constituent of X, with a clutch region [α′, α) and X|[α′,α)(α′′) = X(α′′), for
any ordinal α′′ ∈[α′, α). In particular X|[α′,α) coincides with X for α′ = α0 and
is the remainder of the skand X on a clutch region [α′, α) for each α′ ∈(α0, α).
We also denote sets X(α′) by Xα′, α0 ≤α′ < α, calling them constituents of
the skand X and skands X|[α′,α), α0 ≤α′ < α, calling them parts of the skand
X. It is also clear that all remainders X|[α′,α), α′ ∈(α0, α), of the skand X
are skands indeed with clutch regions [α′, α) and with induced relations of their
constituents and parts that are portion of the relationship and portion of the
constituents of the skand X itself.
It is also clear that a skand X with a clutch region [α0, α) uniquely de-
fines a mapping f : [α0, α) →V[U] if we put f(α′) = X(α′) for each ordinal
α′ ∈[α0, α). This mapping f = FX, where F is the forgetful functor of the
membership relation ∈of the constituents and parts of the skand X, we will
call the mapping associated with the skand X.
We call the length of a skand X with a clutch region [α0, α) an ordinal
number l = α −α0 , i.e., a single ordinal such that α = α0 + l (here + is an
ordinary sum of ordinal numbers as types of well-ordered sets and itis different
from adding of Conway numners), and we will distinguish here between finite
skands whose length are equal to a natural numbers, i.e., the ordinals l ∈(0, ω),
and infinite skands whose length are l ≥ω, where ω is the smallest countable
ordinal number.
To define equality of skands, we need the notions of counting functions and
equality of counting functions.
15


## Page 16


Definition 2. Let f : C′ →V[U] and f ′ : C′ →V[U] be arbitrary mappings
of subclasses C and C′ of the class On of all ordinal numbers in the univer-
sal class V[U] of all sets of the theory NBG[U], which we call them counting
functions. Let’s say that these counting functions f and f′ are equal if there
exists a similar mapping φ : C →C′, i.e., a bijection φ preserving the structure
of well-ordered sets C and C′, which we will call an isomorphism for short for
which the following usual equality of mappings f = f′ ◦φ is satisfied.
We denote this equality of the counting functions f and f′ also by f = f ′,
but distinguish it from the usual equality f = f′ of the mappings f and f ′ when
their domains of definition are equal and for each element from this domain the
values of the mappings on this element are equal.
Proposition 2 The relation of equality of mappings as counting functions
is an equivalence relation.
Proof.
The reflexivity of f = f is obvious, since the identity mapping
φ = 1|C : C →C is an identity isomorphism of a well-ordered class C onto itself
and the usual equality of mappings f = f ◦1|C takes place. Hence, there is
equality of the mapping as a counting function of itself, i.e., f = f. This is the
rare case when the notions of equality of mappings as counting functions and
the usual equality of mappings coincide with each other.
Let’s prove symmetry. Let the mappings f : C →V[U] and f′ : C′ →V[U]
be equal as counting functions, i.e., f = f′. Then, by Definition 2, the usual
equality of mappings f = f′◦φ is satisfied, where φ : C →C′ is an isomorphism
of well-ordered classes.
But since φ−1 : C′ →C is an inverse isomorphism
of well-ordered classes, the formula f = f ′ ◦φ entails the following equality:
f ◦φ−1 = (f ′ ◦φ) ◦φ−1 = f ′ ◦(φ ◦φ−1) = f ′ ◦1|C′ = f ′ of the corresponding
mappings, and hence the equality f ′ = f ◦φ−1 of the mappings is satisfied by
virtue of the symmetry of the equality relation of the mappings. Hence, the
equality f ′ = f of mappings as counting functions is satisfied.
If f ′′ : C′′ →V[U] is another mapping of the class C′′ ⊆On into the
universal class V[U], then the transitivity (f = f′)&(f ′ = f ′′) ⇒(f = f ′′) of
the equality of mappings as counting functions is also obvious. If the premise,
by Definition 2, entails the usual equalities f = f′ ◦φ and f ′ = f ′′ ◦ψ of
the corresponding mappings, where ψ : C′ →C′ is an isomorphism of the well-
ordered classes C′ and C′′, then supposing χ = ψ◦φ, we obtain the isomorphism
χ : C →C′′ of well-ordered classes C and C′′ and the following equality:
f = f ′ ◦φ = (f ′′ ◦ψ) ◦φ = f ′′ ◦(ψ ◦φ) = f ′′ ◦χ mappings, and hence the
equality f = f ′′ ◦χ mappings. Hence, by Definition 2, there is equality f = f′′
of mappings as counting functions.
Definition 3. Two skands X and Y with clutch regions [α0, α) and [β0, β),
respectively, are called equal (we write X = Y ) if the corresponding mappings
f and g associated with skands X and Y are equal as counting functions, i.e.,
there exists a similar mapping φ : [α0, α) →[β0, β) of the intervals [α0, α) and
[β0, β) that preserves the structures of the well-ordered sets [α0, α) and [β0, β),
i.e., the existence of an isomorphism φ of well-ordered sets and the following
usual equality of mappings f = g ◦φ.
16


## Page 17


The description of skands and their equalities can be given less formally,
but more clearly and conveniently for their recording and working with them.
Let’s call this form of a skand unfolded, in the sense that in the record of the
skand itself the region of its coupling will be given and all its elements, or more
precisely, all the relations of membership ∈of its constituents and parts will
already be specified.
Let’s first consider “strictly decreasing“ sequences of nested curly braces
{α0{α0+1...{α′... ...}α′...}α0+1}α0; strictly decreasing in the sense that for any
ordinals α′ < α′′ such that α′, α′′ ∈[α0, α), the pair of brackets {α′′...}α′′“lies
inside“ a pair of brackets {α′...}α′.
The possibility of the existence of such a strictly decreasing sequence of
nested curly braces can “be modeled“ on the sequence of ends of segments
[−1
α′ , 1
α′ ], α′ ∈[α0, α) of Conway numbers [14], where the Conway number −1
α′
corresponds to the open curly brace {α′, and the Conway number 1
α′ corresponds
to the closed curly brace }α′, if of course, α0 ̸= 0. In this very case we put the
Conway number −2 corresponds to the open curly brace {α0, and the Conway
number 2 corresponds to the closed curly brace }α0. And since for any ordinals
α0 ≤α′ < α′′ < α, the closed segment [−1
α′′ ,
1
α′′ ] of all Conway numbers lies
inside the segment [−1
α′ , 1
α′ ], then in the same sense the pair of curly braces
{α′′...}α′′ “lies inside“ a pair of curly brace {α′...}α′.
Remark 1. Note only that unlike Conway numbers, which are mathematical
objects, curly brackets are not at all, but play here a syntactical role, indicating
only the membership relations ∈of constituents and parts of the skand: every-
thing between the corresponding pair of curly braces {α′}α′, where α′ ∈[α0, α)

In other words, a skand X[α0,α) is an aggregate, or a chained decreasing-tuple
of founded sets or individuals, or their absence. A trivial skand is a chained
decreasing-tuple with empty components; an arbitrary skand is a chained decreasing-
tuple with random components that are elements of the universe V[U] of the
founded sets.
(Skand in Sanskrit means “random aggregate of being“.)
Any skand X[α0,α) with components Xα′, α0 ≤α′ < α, Definitions 1′

etc.). We will see that skands are real and natural mathematical objects.

2. Reflexive skands
Definition 4 A skand X[α0,α) is called reflexive if the skand X[α0+1,α) is
equal to the skand X[α0,α), i.e., X[α0+1,α) = X[α0,α).
Proposition 3. A skand X[α0,α) is reflexive if and only if one of the follow-
ing two conditions is satisfied:
1) α −α0 ≥ω and for any ordinal α′ such that α0 < α′ < α0 + ω, there is
an equality Xα′ = Xα0, or, which is the same thing, the mapping f|[α0,α0+ω),
19


## Page 20


which is a restriction to the interval [α0, α0 + ω) of the mapping f : [α0, α) →
V[U] associated with the skand X[α0,α), is a constant mapping whose image
f([α0, α0 + ω)) is the set {Xα0} ⊂V[U];
2) for any ordinal α′ such that α0 < α′ < α0 + ω, the equality of the skands
X[α′,α) = X[α0,α).
Proof. Necessity. Indeed, let the skand X[α0,α) be reflexive. Then, by
Definition 4, X[α0+1,α) = X[α0,α).
Hence, by Definition 2′, first, the intervals [α0 + 1, α) and [α0, α) are sim-
ilar (isomorphic), and hence α −α0 ≥ω. Otherwise, such an isomorphism is
impossible, since any finite interval [α0, α) of ordinal numbers represents the or-
dinal type of a finite ordinal number l = α −α0 different from the finite ordinal
number l −1 represented by the ordinal type of the interval [α0 + 1, α).
Second, there are the following equality of sets: Xα0+1 = Xα0, Xα0+2 =
Xα0+1,...,Xα′+1 = Xα′,... for any ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α0 + ω). By the transitivity
of the set equality relation, we obtain the following formula: Xα′ = Xα0 for all
ordinals α′ such that α0 < α′ < α0 + ω.
It is also obvious that the restriction f|[α0,α0+ω) of the mapping f : [α0, α) →
V[U] associated with the reflexive skand X[α0,α), on the set [α0, α0 + ω), is a
constant mapping, whose image is the set {Xα0} ⊂V[U], or equivalently, the
value f(α′) = Xα0 for any ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α0 + ω).
The converse is also true, i.e., constancy of the restriction f|[α0,α0+ω) of the
mapping f : [α0, α) →V[U] associated with the reflexive skand X[α0,α), on the
set [α0, α0 + ω), whose image is the set {Xα0} ⊂V[U], entails equality of the
sets Xα′ = Xα0 for any α0 < α′ < α0 + ω. Thus the necessity of condition 1) is
proved.
Let’s now show that condition 1) entails the equality X[α′,α) = X[α0,α) for
any ordinal α′ such that α0 < α′ < α0 + ω.
Indeed, we associate for each
ordinal α′′ ∈[α′, α0 + ω), an ordinal α′′ −1 ∈[α0, α), and for each ordinal
α′′ ∈[α0 + ω, α) we associate this ordinal α′′ ∈[α0, α) itself and obtain an
isomorphism of well-ordered sets [α′, α) and [α0, α). Thus the corresponding
components of Xα′′ of skands X[α′,α) and X[α0,α) skands are equal to each
other: for ordinals α′′ ∈[α′, α0 + ω) and α′′ ∈[α0, α0 + ω), respectively, they
are equal to the set Xα0, and for each ordinal α′′ ∈[α0 + ω, α) ⊂[α′, α) and
α′′ ∈[α0 + ω, α) ⊂[α0, α), respectively, the components Xα′′ and Xα′′ of the
skands X[α′,α) and X[α′,α), respectively, are equal to each other, i.e., Xα′′ = Xα′′.
Thus the necessity of the condition 2) is proved.
Sufficiency. If condition 1) of Proposition 3 is satisfied, then, first, α′ −
α0 ≥ω, and hence there is an isomorphism φ of well-ordered sets [α0 + 1, α)
and [α0, α), we associate for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0 + 1, α0 + ω), the ordinal
φ(α′) = α′ −1 ∈[α0, α0 + ω), and for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0 + ω, α) we associate
the same ordinal φ(α′) = α′ ∈[α0 + ω, α), what is the desired isomorphism of
φ : [α0 + 1, α) →[α0, α) since [α0 + 1, α) = [α0 + 1, α0 + ω) ⊔[α0 + ω, α) and
[α0, α) = [α0, α0 + ω) ⊔[α0 + ω, α), and the mapping φ defined above preserves
in an obvious way the structure of the well-ordered sets [α0 + 1, α) and [α0, α).
Second, condition 1) entails the equality Xα′ = Xα0 for any ordinal α′ ∈
[α0 + 1, α0 + ω), and hence, the equality X[α0+1,α) = X[α0,α), since for any

component Xα′, α0 + 1 ≤α′ < α0 + ω, skand X[α0+1,α′, and any component
Xα′, α0 ≤α′ < α0 + ω, of the skand X[α0,α), there is an equality Xα′ = Xα0,
and the components of Xα′ of the skands X[α0+1,α) and X[α0,α) for all ordinals

If condition 2) of Proposition 3 is satisfied, then the equality X[α0+1,α) =

3. Self-similar skands
Definition 5.
An infinite skand X[α0,α) is called self-similar if for each
ordinal α′ such that α0 < α′ < α, the skand X[α′,α) is equal to the skand
X[α0,α), i.e., X[α′,α) = X[α0,α).
Proposition 4. A skand X[α0,α) is self-similar if and only if α −α0 = ωκ,
0 < κ < Ω, and thus for any ordinal α′, α0 < α′ < α, there is an equality of the
sets Xα′ = Xα0 or, similarly, the mapping f : [α0, α) →V[U] associated with
the skand X[α0,α) is constant.
Proof.
Necessity.
By Definition 5, for any ordinal α′ such that α0 <
α′ < α, there is an equality of the skands X[α′,α) = X[α0,α), and hence an
isomorphism φ : [α′, α) →[α0, α) of the intervals [α′, α) and [α0, α). Since the
formula ψ(α′′) = α0+α′′, for any ordinal α′′ ∈[0, α−α0), gives the isomorphism
ψ : [0, α −α0) →[α0, α) of the intervals [0, α −α0) and [α0, α), then for any
residue ρ of the ordinal α−α0 (i.e., such an ordinal ρ that α−α0 = σ+ρ for some
ordinal σ) there exists an ordinal α′′ ∈[0, α −α0) such that ρ is defined by the
interval [α′′, α −α0) and vice versa: any ordinal α′′ such that 0 < α′′ < α −α0
defines the residue ρ of the ordinal [0, α−α0) by the interval [ρ, α−α0). But for
any ordinal α′ = ψ(α′′) there is an isomorphism of the intervals φ : [α′, α) →
[α0, α) and hence the isomorphism ψ−1 ◦φ ◦ψ : [α′′, α −α0) →[0, α −α0)
intervals [α′′, α −α0) and [0, α −α0), and hence the equality of the numbers ρ
and α −α0.
And if any residue ρ of the ordinal number α −α0 is equal to this number
α−α0, then it happens if and only if α−α0 = ωκ, κ ≥1, (see: [22], Chap. VII,
§7, Theorem 7).
The equality of the skands X[α′,α) = X[α0,α), for any ordinal α′ such that
α0 < α′ < α, by Definition 2′, entails equality of the first components of these
skands, i.e., Xα′ = Xα0, for any ordinal α0 < α′ < α. And since Xα′ = f(α′),
where f : [α0, α) →V[U] is the mapping associated with the skand X[α0,α), it is
a constant mapping, whose image f([α0, α)) is the subset {Xα0} of the universal
class V[U].
Sufficiency. Let α −α0 = ωκ, 0 < κ ≤Ω. Then by the already mentioned
Theorem 7 of [22], Chap. VII, §7, and the above, we obtain an isomorphism
of the intervals [α0, α) and [α′, α) for any α′, α0 < α′ < α. And since the
mapping f : [α0, α) →V[U] associated with the skand X[α0,α) is constant, then,
by Definition 2′, for any α′, α0 < α′ < α, we obtain an equality of the skands
X[α′,α) = X[α0,α). □
Clearly, any self-similar skand X[α0,α) is reflexive, but not vice versa. For
a self-similar skand X[α0,α), the ordinal α −α0 ≥ω must be a limit ordinal of
21


## Page 22


the form α −α0 = ωκ, κ ≥1, and for the reflexive skand X[α0,α) the ordinal
α −α0 ≥ω can be either a limit of any kind or a non-limit ordinal number.
4. Periodic skands
Definition 6. A skand X[α0,α) is called weakly periodic with period τ =
ωξ1 · n1 + ωξ2 · n2 + ... + ωξr · nr, where ξ1 > ξ2 > ... > ξr, 0 ≤ni < ω for each
natural numbers ni, 0 < i ≤r, if τ is the smallest ordinal 0 < τ < Ωsuch that
for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α0 + ωξ1+1) the equality X[α′,α) = X[α′(+)τ,α) holds;
it is called periodic if for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α) the skand X[α′,α) weakly
periodic with the same period τ; and it is called strictly periodic if it is periodic
and for any ordinal λ = α0 + ωξ1+1 · κ ∈[α0, α), 0 < κ < κ(α), the equality
X[α0,α) = X[λ,α) holds.
Remark 2. One has distinguish here and further so-called a natural sum
of ordinal numbers α′(+)τ and a usual sum α0 + ωξ1+1 of ordinal numbers. In
general, they are different (a natural sum (+) is commutative, at that time a
usual sum + is not commutative). Definitions of natural sum (+) and of natural
product (·) see in [22] Chap. VII, § and [19], p. 591-594. Notice only that for
any ordinals ν and µ one hasf ν(+)µ = max{ν + µ, µ + ν}, where + is a usual
sum of ordinal numbers, which, clearly, for finite ordinals 0 < ν < ω coincides
with a natural sum for every µ (finite or infinite ordinals).

Remark 3. If α −α0 = ω, then all three concepts – weak periodicity, peri-
odicity, and strict periodicity – coincide. If α−α0 > ω, then all three concepts –
weak periodicity, periodicity and strict periodicity are different, generally speak-
ing. Clearly, that a strictly periodic skand is periodic and a periodic skand is
weakly periodic but in both case non vica virsa. Indeed, a weakly periodic skand
X[α0,α) such that for some α0 + ω ≤λ′ < α a skand X[α′,α) ̸= X[α′′,α) for every
ordinal α0 ≤α′′ < α0 + ω, is not periodic. Similarly, a periodic skand X[α0,α)
such that for some α0 + ω ≤λ′ < α a skand X[α′,α) ̸= X[α′′,α) for every ordinal
α0 ≤α′′ < α0 +ω, is not strictly periodic. A strictly periodic skand with period
τ = 1 is a reflexive skand and a strictly periodic skand with period τ = 1 is a
self-similar skand, and in both cases vice versa. The notion of strict periodicity
so defined here was applied [21] to extend the definition of the trigonometric
functions sin x and cos x to of all Conway numbers x ∈No, which were orig-
inally defined by Conway only for such x ∈No for which there is a natural
number n such that −n < x < n.

5. The elementary formal theory NBG[U](1) of non-founded sets and
its model in the universe U[U](1)
Let’s now define an extension NBG[U](1) of the formal theory NBG[U]−,
where U can be either proper class of individuals or a set of individuals in
particular the empty set, and then we obtain the theory of “pure non-founded
sets“ NBG(1)mentioned above in the Abstract and Introduction.
By NBG[U](1), we mean the formal theory NBG[U]−with two more axioms
attached to its own axioms.
Skand existence axiom (SEA). For any ordinals α0, α ∈On such that
α −α0 ≥ω, and any mapping f : [α0, α) →V[U] there exists a skand X[α0,α),
for which the equality Xα′ = f(α′) holds for every ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α), and it
belongs to the universe X[α0,α) ∈V[U]−, i.e., the set Xα0∪{X[α0+1,α)} ∈V[U]−.
Remark 4.
The class of all such sets Xα0 ∪{X[α0+1,α)} we denote by
V[U](1) if we interpret V[U] = V[U](0). If the length l = α −α0 of the skand
X[α0,α) is finite, then the set X[α0,α) = Xα0 ∪{X[α0+1,α)} is founded; if the
length is infinite, then the set X[α0,α) = Xα0 ∪{X[α0+1,α)} is non-founded but
pseudo-founded what arises from the following axiom. Notice only that any
set X ∈V[U] can be represented as a finite skand X[α0,α), with the length
α −α0 = 1. Indeed, if X = {x0, x1, ..., xλ, ...}, 0 ≤λ < Λ, is a founded set,
then the skand X[α0,α0+1) with the length α −α0 = 1 and Xα0 = X is a desired
skand.
Pseudo-founding Axiom (PFA). For each set X ∈V[U](1), any maximal
descending ∈-sequence X ∋x1 ∋x2 ∋x3 ∋... ∋xn−1 ∋xn ∋.... has the
following property: if the sequence is finite, then its last element xn is either
the empty set ∅or an individual u ∈U; if it is infinite, then there exists the
smallest number n such that xn is a skand of infinite length.
The latter means that xn = X[α0,α) for some ordinals α0, α ∈On such that
l = α −α0 ≥ω.
In the case when all maximal descending ∈-sequences in X are finite, then
the set X is obviously founded, and otherwise, when at least one ∈-sequence is
infinite, then the set X is non-founded, but obviously is pseudo-founded, i.e., the
∈-sequence is finite in the sense that its last term is a skand of infinite length.

Exact extensionality axiom (EEA). (∀X)(∀Y )[X = Y ⇒(∀Z)(X ∈
Z) ⇒Y ∈Z), where sets X, Y, Z ∈V[U](1) (the elements of the sets can be
individuals, founded sets, and unfounded sets) and X and Y are equal , if for
each element x ∈X, there exists an element y ∈Y such that x = y, and for
each element y ∈Y , there exists an element x ∈X such that y = x, where the
equality “=“ means the following:.
28


## Page 29


1) the equality of individuals x, y ∈U, i.e., (x = y) ⇔(∀Z)(x ∈Z ⇔y ∈Z);
2) the equality of founded sets Z, Z′ ∈V[U](0), i.e., by equality in Extension-
ality axiom EA in NBG[U](0) (i.e., two founded sets are equal if they contain
the same elements);
3) the equality of skands when x, y ∈S′[U], i.e., by Definition 2′;
4) the equality of sets when x, y /∈U and x, y /∈V[U](0), i.e., the subsequent
iteration using 1), 2), 3) in EES.
By virtue of the axiom PFA, for every element x, y in 4), such an iteration is
finite and ends up with either an empty set, an individual, or an infinite skand
to which the rules 2), 1), and 3) apply, respectively.
Remark 5. The last condition means, for example, that if a set x contains
an element such that it contains as its elements individuals, founded sets and
infinite skands, then a set y must have the same element containing equal in-
dividuals, founded sets and infinite skands, respectively, and vice versa. This
brings us back to the usual definition of an equality of sets: sets X are equal to
Y if they consist of the same elements. A priori, if in the universe V[U] there
are two non-founded sets, e.g., X ∈X and Y ∈Y , then their equality X = Y
without a specific definition of reflexive sets is impossible by the definition of
equality in the extensionality axiom EA, viz. i.e. by the formula (1), since it
reduces to the tautology X = Y ⇔X = Y , but in (EEA) this equality agrees
with the fact that equal sets do consist of the same elements and vice versa.
The last class of sets V[U](1) is defined by the following transfinite recur-

The model of the theory NBG[U](1) is constructed as follows: by formulas
(15) and (16) we define a class H[U′] which is a model for the theory NBG[U′].
We now apply the operator of “remembering“ R = F −1 to all individuals
from U(1), which are constituents of the sets in V[U′] = H[U′] \ U(0) or to
the individuals themselves from H[U′] \ U(0).
In other words, in all objects
X ∈H[U′] \ U′(0) we change all individuals from U(1) to their corresponding
skands, then treated as sets, which satisfy all axioms of the theory of NBG[U]−
except the foundation axiom, and by the axiom SEA contained in the universe
V[U]−and are non-founded sets. Indeed, each individual FX[α0,α) becomes a
set X[α0,α) containing a set X[α0+1,α), which in turn contains a set X[α0+2,α),
and so on: X(α0,α) ∋X[α0+1,α) ∋X[α0+2,α) ∋... since the skand X[α0,α) has
infinite length l = α −α0 ≥ω.
It is easy to see that after such changes, the class V[U′] of the theory
NBG[U′] turns into the class V[U](1) of sets of both the NBG[U](1)-founded
and non-founded theory NBG[U](1).
Moreover, it follows from Paragraph 1
that U[U](1) = V[U](1) ∪U(0) is a model for NBG[U](1). (For details, see [28],
p. 282-283, 302).
These definitions and the description of the theory NBG[U](1) lead to the
following theorem.
Theorem 1. The theory NBG[U](1) is consistent, provided of course that
the theory NBG−is consistent.
Proof. It is well known that if NBG−is consistent, then the extension
NBG of this theory is also consistent, obtained by attaching to it the founding
axiom FA as the only new axiom. (See [28] Proposition 4.45 and Corollary 4.86,
p. 283.) Bernays proved in [4] (using a more complicated model than the one
considered here, and proving the compatibility of the FA-founding axiom) that
this axiom is independent of all other axioms of the NBG−theory. So both the
axiom FA and its negation of the axioms SEA and PFA can be attached to
the NBG−-theory without contradiction.
It follows from Proposition 1 that NBG−and NBG are consistent if and
only if NBG[U]−and NBG[U] are respectively consistent. Given the above, the
consistency of NBG[U]−follows from the consistency of NBG[U]. Similarly, by
Bernays, the regularity axiom RA, which in the presence of CA is equivalent
to the founding axiom FA, is independent of all other axioms of the theory
NBG[U]−. Therefore, joining the axioms SEA and PFA that negate the axiom
30

FA to NBG[U]−leads to a consistent theory NBG[U](1) if of course the theory
NBG[U]−itself is consistent. The latter is consistent if the theory NBG−is
consistent (See details in [31] or Chap. 4, Proposition 4.50 in [28].) □
Since ∅⊂U, from the axiom PFA and the formulas (15) and (16), we get
the following inclusions:
V ⊂V[U] = V[U](0) ⊂V[U](1) ⊂V[U]−.

6. ν-generalized skands, the formal theory NBG[U](ν) and its model
in the universal U[U](ν)
In the previous paragraph, we extended the formal set theory of NBG[U]−
by attaching two axioms SEA and PFA which are less restrictive, than the
founding axiom FA in NBG[U], and we obtained a new formal theory of already
non-founded sets NBG[U](1) in contrast to the theory of NBG[U] where all sets
are founded. We also extended this class of all founded sets V[U] = V[U](0)
by using infinite length skands, which are essentially non-founded sets, to the
universal class of all sets V[U](1) of NBG[U](1) theory, with V[U](1) ⊂V[U]−,
and increased the class of elements U[U](1) = V[U](1) ∪U of this theory, which
is a model for it. We can naturally continue such a process of extending the
theory NBG[U]−for every ordinal number ν ∈On, ν > 1, defining inductively
the theories NBG[U](ν′), 1 ≤ν′ ≤ν, extending V[U](0) with new ν′-generalized
skands of infinite length, 1 ≤ν′ ≤ν. Moreover, there is the following sequence
of inclusions:
V[U](0) ⊂V[U](1) ⊂V[U](2) ⊂... ⊂V[U](ν) ⊂V[U]−.
(33)
We do this by the following transfinite induction.
Let ν be an arbitrary ordinal such that ν > 1. Suppose we have already
constructed formal set theories NBG[U](ν′) for every ν′, 1 ≤ν′ < ν, universal
classes V[U](ν′) and U[U](ν′) of all sets and all elements of these theories, respec-
tively, by means of new objects ν′′- generalized skands X(ν′′) ∈S′[U](ν′′) of infi-
nite length, 1 ≤ν′′ ≤ν′, and U[U](ν′′) are models of the theories NBG[U](ν′′),
1 ≤ν′′ ≤ν′.
Now, to construct a formal set theory NBG[U](ν) and the class V[U](ν) of
all sets of this theory with the universal class of elements U[U](ν) = V[U](ν) ∪U,
which will represent a model of the theory NBG[U](ν), we need the following
definitions of the new objects.

Theorem 2 The theory NBG[U](ν) is consistent, provided of course that the

The formal theory of non-founded sets NBG[U](Ω) is a first-order theory
with its own axioms NBG[U]−and attached to them two axioms Ω-SAE and
Ω-PFA with a refinement of the exact extensional axiom Ω-EEA.
The axiom Ω-SAE is that for any ordinals α0, α ∈On such that α−α0 ≥ω,
and for any mapping f : [α0, α) →V[U](Ω) there exists an ordinal ν, 1 ≤ν < Ω,
such that a ν-generalized skand X(ν) has the following property: X(ν)(α′) =
f(α′), for each ordinal α0 ≤α′ < α.
The axiom Ω-PFA is that for any set X, any maximal descending ∈-sequence
X ∋x1 ∋x2 ∋x3 ∋... ∋xn−1 ∋xn ∋... has the following property: if
the sequence is finite, then its last element xn is either an empty set ∅or an
individual u ∈U; if it is infinite, then there exists such a smallest number n
that xn is a ν-generalized skand of infinite length for some ordinal 1 ≤ν ≤Ω.
The axiom Ω-EEA is the following: (∀X)(∀Y )(∀Z)(X) = Y ∧X ∈Z ⇒
Y ∈Z), where the two sets X and Y , whose elements can be individuals,
founded sets and non-founded sets, as ν-generalized skands, are equal, if for
every element x ∈X there exists an element y ∈Y such that x = y and for
every element y ∈Y there exists an element x ∈X such that y = x, where by
the equality “=“ we mean the following:
1) the equality of individuals when x, y ∈U(0), i.e., (x = y) ⇔(∀z)(x ∈z ⇔
y ∈z);
2) the equality of the founded sets when x, y ∈V[U](0), i.e., by the equality
of sets in the extensionality axiom EA in NBG[U];
3) the equality of Ω-generalized skands when x, y ∈S′[U](Ω);
4) the equality of sets in x, y ∈V[U](Ω) when x, y /∈U, x, y /∈V[U](0) and
x, y /∈S′[U](Ω), i.e., the next iteration using 1), 2), 3) in Ω-EEA.

Part two
1. Definition of coskands
The notion of a skand discussed above allows us to dualize it and obtain a new
mathematical object, which in a special case was not previously considered as
definable, e.g., see: “property of an expression having the form ...{...{{0}}...}...
is not “definable“, (see the translator’s comment in [13], footnote 3), p. 109).
Here it will be a well-defined object as an individual when system of pairs of
curly braces ...{...{{0}}...}... has exactly ω pieces and when it has ω + 1 pieces,
i.e., {...{...{{0}}...}...}, it is a one-element set whose element is a trivial coskand
...{...{{0}}...}....
The axioms of set theory NBG[U] do not, in general, specify whether the
class U of individuals is a set or a proper class, except that it follows from the
axioms that there exists a proper class U[U] of all elements; the existence of the
class U or, equivalently, the existence of the universal class V[U], is given by a
separate axiom.
A sufficient number (abundance) of individuals is provided, for example, by
the axiom of plenitude (AP).
Axiom of Plenitude for the class U.
For every set S there exists an
injection f : S →U whose image f(S) is disjoint from S, [3], p. 23.
Axiom of Plenitude as it stands does not guarantee that the collections we
will need do exist. For this purpose there is the following stronger form.
36


## Page 37


Strong Axiom of Plenitude for the class U.
There is an operation
new(a, b) so that
1. For all sets a and all subsets, b ⊆U, new(a, b) ∈U \ b.
2. For all a ̸= a′ and all b ⊆U, new(a, b) ̸=new(a′, b), see: [3], p. 23.
The existence coskand axiom bellow will provide a huge and diverse
number (abundance) of well-distinguished individuals, for which the usual axiom
of choice will be sufficient. The importance of the existence of a sufficient number
of individuals was necessary to prove the independence of the axiom of choice
from the other axioms.
For a symmetric statement of the theory of coskands, we will below fix some
class U of individuals, which may be an empty, finite and infinite set, or a proper
class and it does not matter for the initial definitions but without individuals
we will intruduce now, i.e., without coskands.
Definition 9 Coskand with a clutch region [α0, α) is an object X such that
it associates uniquely for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α) a set X(α′) ∈V[U] and has
the following internal structure of the membership relation ∈of its constituents
and parts: for each ordinal α′ ∈[α0, α) and each element x ∈X(α′) there is
a relation x ∈X|[α0,α′+1) and for each ordinal α′ ∈(α0, α) there is a relation
X|[α0,α′) ∈X|[α0,α′+1), where X|[α0,α′) is the restriction of X to the interval
[α0, α′) and X|[α0,α′+1) is the restriction of X to the interval [α0, α′ + 1).
For each ordinal α′ ∈(α0, α) the coskand X|[α0,α′) will be called a partial
coskand of X. We also denote sets X(α′) by Xα′, α0 ≤α′ < α, calling them
constituents of the coskand X and coskands X|[α0,α′), α0 < α′ < α, we call parts
of the coskand X. It is also clear that all partial coskands X|[α0,α′), α′ ∈(α0, α),
of the coskand X are real coskands with clutch regions [α0, α′) and with induced
relations of their constituents and parts that are portions of the relationship and
portions of the constituents of the coskand X itself.
It is also clear that a coskand X with a clutch region [α0, α) uniquely defines
a mapping f : [α0, α) →V[U] if we put f(α′) = X(α′) for each ordinal α′ ∈
[α0, α). We will call this mapping f the mapping associated with the coskand
X.
Let’s call the length of a caskand X with a clutch region [α0, α) the ordinal
number l = α −α0 and distinguish here between finite caskands, which have
length equal to an ordinal l ∈(0, ω), clearly, being usual sets in V[U] and infinite
coskands which have length l ≥ω, where ω is the smallest countable ordinal,
being new objects.
Definition 10.
Two coskands X and Y with clutch regions [α0, α) and
[β0, β), respectively, are called equal, we write X = Y , if their corresponding
mappings f and g associated to the coskands X and Y are equal as counting
functions, i.e., f = g ◦φ, where φ : [α0, α) →[β0, β) is an isomorphism of
well-ordered sets.
The description of coskands and their equalities can be given in a less formal
way, but in a more visual and convenient way for their recording and working
with them. Let’s call this form of a coskand as unfolded, in the sense that in the
record of the caskand itself the clutch region will be given and all its elements,

will be specified.

2. Reflexive sets and Mirimanoff extraordinary sets in NBG[U](Ω).
The axioms of set theory NBG[U]−are compatible with (see [13], p. 117)
non-founded sets, which include the so-called reflexive sets and the Mirimanoff
extraordinary sets [29].
Definition 11. A set X ∈V[U]−is called is reflexive if one of its elements
is X itself, i.e., X ∈X. Otherwise, the set X is called is non-reflexive, i.e., it is
not its element, i.e., X /∈X.
Definition 12. A set X ∈V[U]−is called is extraordinary in the sense
of Mirimanoff [29] if there exists at least one of its elements x0 with infinite
descending ∈-sequence X ∋x0 ∋x1 ∋x2 ∋... . Otherwise, the set X is called
ordinal, i.e., any maximal descending ∈-sequence X ∋x0 ∋x1 ∋x2 ∋xn ends
either the empty set or an individual, i.e., xn = ∅∨u, u ∈U.
Already the theory of NBG[U](1) is enough to explain the indeterminacy
of the admissible relation X ∈X and the indeterminacy of the Mirimanoff
definition of an extraordinary set, if by default, one does not assume that the
Mirimanoff set is representable as a skand X = X[α0,α) of length l = α−α0 = ω.

A reflexive set X of the universe of sets V[U]−can be represented as a
solution of the following “implicit equation“ Mirimanoff [30], p. 34:
X = {x0, x1, x2, ..., xλ, ..., X},
(41)
where x0, x1, x2, ..., xλ, ... are elements from U[U]−such that {x0, x1, x2, ..., xλ, ....} ∈
V[U]−, or their absence, in which {} = ∅∈V[U]−is trivial, and X is an inde-
pendent variable taking values in V[U]−.
The following natural question arises: “Is the solution of the equation (41)
unambiguous, or is it ambiguous and we need to find all solutions of this equa-
tion? In other words, do the elements x0, x1, x2, ..., xλ, ... in the equation (41)
uniquely determine the set X or not? The answer to this question depends
essentially on one or another theory of non-founded sets. For example, from
the anti-founded axiom AFA of Aczel’s [1], p. 10, follows from the uniqueness
of the solution of the equation (41), although Aczel did not exclude that, in
general, the equation (41) can have many different solutions in particular for
the simplest equation he considered, X = {X} (in our case the set represented
by the empty skand e[α0,α) of length l = α −α0 ≥ω), cf. : [1], p. 6-7.
Let’s now consider the simplest case of the definition of a reflexive set in for-
mal set theory NBG[U](1), i.e., the case where in the equation (41) the elements
x0, x1, x2, ..., xλ, ... belong to the universe U[U] so that {x0, x1, x2, ..., xλ, ...} ∈
V[U], or they are absent, then trivially {} = ∅∈V[U], and X is an independent
variable taking values in V[U](1). In this case, we will briefly say that there is
an equation (41) in theory NBG[U](1)whose solutions are reflexive sets X of the
universe V[U](1).
Similarly, we can talk about the equation (41) in the theory NBG[U](ν),
2 ≤ν ≤Ωand its solutions if the elements x0, x1, x2, ..., xλ, ... belong to the uni-

Since any solution X of equation (41) is a reflexive set, and V[U](0) is the

It is known that the class V[U](0) is not a set, but is a proper class (we will
give a new proof of this fact, which does not use the Mirimanoff or Russell
antinomies, in the next paragraph), then the set of all solutions of the equation
(41) in the theory NBG[U](1) is not a proper class, since the parameters of this
family contain arbitrary elements from V[U](0).
Recall here the definition of a parametric family of sets.
A mapping f of a set E onto a set F is called a parametric representation of
a set F by means of a set E whose elements are called parameters. Moreover,
a parametric representation of any subset of set F is equivalent to specifying
a mapping of some set E into F. (See [8], p. 364-365). Let’s add only one
clarification: If the mapping f of the set E “into“ (respectively “onto“) F is
injective, then the subset f(E) (respectively the set F itself) is parametrized by
the set of parameters E and is representable as S
e∈E
{f(e)}; if the mapping f is
not injective, then the parameterized set F
e∈E
{f(e)} is actually the set F
e∈E
{f(e)}.
In our special case, we extend this definition of a parametrized family of sets to
arbitrary classes E and F, which can be (and in our case are) proper classes.
Indeed, as F we suppose a subclass of all reflexive sets X ∈V[U](1) with the
same subsets X\{X}, and as E we suppose all reflexive skands X[α0,α) for which
Xα′ = X \ {X}, α0 ≤α′ < ω, and we map to each such skand X[α0,α) ∈E a
reflexive set {x0, x1, ...xλ, ..., X[α0+1,α)}.

3. Naive and formal notions of reflexive and Mirimanoff
extraordinary sets
Reflexive sets in an implicit and unarticulated form first appeared in 1899
by Georg Cantor, the founder of set theory, when he distinguished a consistent
multiplicity, or such a definable multiplicity, which has the property that the
assumption of “coexistence of all its elements does not lead to a contradiction
and it can be regarded as a unity, as some “completed or final thing“, and he also
distinguished a inconsistent plurality, i.e., such plurality, which has the property
that the assumption of “coexistence“ of all its elements leads to a contradiction
46


## Page 47


and it cannot be regarded as a unity as some “completed or final thing“ [32]
(Cantor’s letter to Dedekind from Halle, July 28, 1899). To paraphrase Cantor,
a inconsistent multiplicity is an incomplete thing that cannot be fixed or defined
in any way. It is a inconsistent multiplicity that formally contained itself as its
element, but since it were contradictory and, consequently, non-existent, this
reflexivity of theirs turned out to be merely fictitious.
Examples of such inconsistent sets are, according to Cantor, the set (collec-
tion) U of all sets M, the system Ωof all ordinal numbers α and the system S
of all cardinal numbers a. It is easy to see that all these multiplicities, assuming
their existence, contain themselves as their own element, which after simple rea-
soning leads to a contradiction, although their contradictory nature was proved
differently by Cantor. This contradiction indicates only that such entities sim-
ply do not exist. Therefore, they cannot be considered as examples of reflexive
sets, contrary to the standard example of the set of all sets, “For other sets,
one would as little hesitate to regard them as being members of themselves; the
set of all sets is an obvious example“, according to the authors of the famous
monograph [18], p. 5.
Indeed, the set of all sets contains itself as an element by the definition of
U ∈U, or because, for example, it contains as its subset the set of all its subsets,
i.e., 2U ⊂U, which leads to a contradiction by Cantor’s famous theorem about a
cardinality of all subsets of a set, and thus makes the multiplicity U inconsistent.
If Ωwere a consistent multiplicity, Cantor showed that it would be a well-
ordered multiplicity to which corresponds some single ordinal number δ, and
then by virtue of the maximality of Ω, the ordinal δ would be contained in Ω
and would be greater than all ordinal numbers, and thus greater than itself,
i.e., δ < δ, [32] (Cantor’s letter to Dedekind from Halle, July 28, 1899), but
following von Neumann the ordinal numbers are founded sets ordered by the ∈
membership relation, we obtain the implicit presence in Cantor’s proof of the
relation δ ∈δ, since δ < δ, the system Ωis a reflexive set, without the relative
fact that it does not exist in this particular case.
Similarly, Cantor showed that if Ma is any definite set of the class a, a ∈S,
and S were a set, then the discrete union T = F
a∈S
Ma of all sets Ma, each of
which corresponds in power to one and only one cardinal number a ∈S, and
hence T would belong to some class, say, a0. But by Cantor’s famous theorem
a′
0 = 2a0 > a0, which means that the set T would contain as a part a set, for
example, Ma′
0 = 2T of greater power a0, which is a contradiction according to
Cantor. Hence, the multiplicity T as well as the multiplicity S are inconsistent.
What is important for us here is that in Cantor’s theorem Ma′
0 = 2T ∋T; and
since Ma′

Cantor himself. But this unarticulated formula implies that T is a reflexive set.
True, the contradiction obtained by Cantor says only that such a system T is
not a consistent multiplicity and is not a completed or final thing or unity that is
a mathematical object. See [32] (Cantor’s letter to Dedekind from Hahnenklee,
August 31, 1899).
Later, reflexive sets appeared in the same fictitious way in the well-known
47


## Page 48


set-theoretic paradoxes – Zermelo, Russell, Mirimanoff, Hilbert and others.
Let’s first note the original definition of the paradoxical paradox of Russell’s
class w, which caused in 1903 a painful reaction of almost all mathematicians
and especially tragic for Gottlieb Frege.
It’s Russell’s famous phrase: “We
consider a contradiction arising from the obvious fact that if w is the class of all
such classes which as individual members are not elements of themselves, then
it can be proved that w as some object is and is not an element of itself“. [35],
Chap. X, p. 107. Thus. the following paradoxical formula of Russell is already
explicitly articulated here:
w ∈w ⇔w /∈w,
(45)
which says that the class w is a reflexive class with respect to the membership
relation ∈if and only if it is not reflexive. Thus even here the reflexive mul-
tiplicity is fictitious, since Russell’s class w is an inconsistent multiplicity and
therefore does not exist, and this can be shown (see below) without reference
to Russell’s paradox. Moreover, not knowing how to resolve his paradox, Rus-
sell writes later directly about any set: “The relation X ∈X must always be
meaningless“, [38], p. 81, and later clarifies: “Thus X ∈X must be considered
meaningless, since ∈requires that the inclusive be a class composed of objects
of the same type as itself“, ([35], Chap. X, p. 107). In this case, elementary
logical reasoning compels Russell to state that: “X ̸∈X must always also be
meaningless“([38], p. 81). He wrote: “If α is a class, then the statement ‘α is not
an element of the class α’ is always meaningless, so there is no meaning in the
phrase “the class of those classes which are not members of themselves“ ([38], p.
66.) Specifically: “A class consisting of only one element cannot be identified
with that element“. And then instantly adds: “the class X = {X} must be
absolutely meaningless, not simply false“, [38], p. 81.
The reason for such “fictitiousness“ of reflexive and more generally non-
founded sets was and often still is the naive idea that it is possible, by some
condition or premise like “the existence of the totality of all sets“, to draw a false
conclusion that this totality is an element of itself. Even at the present time,
not all mathematicians clearly understand how to correctly construct a reflexive
or non-founded set, since in classical monographs on set theory, such sets are
almost ignored. A good example is the formal construction of non-founded sets
in such a serious monograph as “Set theory and the continuum hypothesis“ by
Paul J. Cohen with a characteristic comment: “If non-founded sets exist, then
they must be objects of a very artificial nature. Let’s show how you can build a
world containing such sets. Let x1, x2, ... be formal symbols; let’s formally put
xn+1 ∈xn and xi /∈xj, at i ̸= j + 1. Let R(0) = {x1, x2, ...}. We define R(α)
by recursion as the set-degree for S
β<α
R(β). The objects in R(α) for α > 0 are
sets, whereas R(0) consist only of xi. Now, for the elements of sets R(α), we
define some ∈-relation as follows: if u is a set, then v ∈u, if v is an element of
u; if u = xi, then v ∈u, if v = xi+1. The axioms are checked quite simply“.
(See [12], Chap. II, § 5.)
There is a minor inaccuracy in this construction of non-founded sets since

the axiom of extensionality is not fulfilled, because the sets {xj+1} and xj,
0 < j < ω, are equal, but in Cohen’s construction they are different elements. In
fact, Cohen’s construction can be imagined in a more natural way. Since, by the
definition of a set, xj = {xj+1} for each 0 < j < ω, then x1 = {x2} = {{x3}} =
... . That’s in the language of the skand the set x1, by default, is the empty
skand e[1,ω) = {1{2{...}2}1. The only difference is that the equality of Cohen’s
symbols xj, 0 < j < ω, and the equality of the skands are different because
the skand e[1,ω) is reflexive, while Cohen’s set is not reflexive. If we consider a
“strong equality“ of skands e[j,ω) = {j{j+1{...}}j+1}j, 0 < j < ω as equalities
of ordered sequences (j, j+1, j+2, ...), then e[j,ω) = {j{j+1{...}}j+1}j = e[i,ω) =
{i{i+1{...}}i+1}i if and only if j = i and hence xj = xi if and only if j = i like
in Cohen’s construction. Nevertheless, since Cohen does not specify, by default,
what the symbols xi, 0 < i < ω themselves mean, then in his construction they
are not at all unambiguous, i.e., a variety of non-founded sets can correspond
to the same characters, and the totality of such sets makes up a proper class
like e[1,α), ω < α < Ω.
Remark 7. All of the above is done in the logic of the mathematicians of
the time mentioned. In fact, many statements do not correspond to reality. For
example, an indication that the totality of all sets, all ordinals, all cardinals, etc.,
i.e., inconsistent multiplicities in Cantor’s terminology, are contradictory. This
is how Cantor and other mathematicians of that time thought. In fact, this is
not the case. These multiplicative formations are simply not definable, i.e., they
are not mathematical objects: they do not exist, but subsist (bestehen, Ger).
They don’t exist, they mean. The difference in this case can be described as the
difference between the non-existence of a number that is not equal to itself, i.e.,
there is no such number! And the totality of all Conway numbers, which does
not exist in the sense of a complete process of their formation, but there is any
number individually or there is a plurality of numbers (sets of numbers). This
is the same difference between an object and a process, between a constant and
a proportional form, between a number and a sequence that converges in some
metric to this number, in particular, a constant sequence (aα)0≤α<λ, such that
for each ordinal α ∈[0, λ) there is an equality aα = a, and the number a itself,
etc.
4. Known “paradoxes“ cannot be derived in formal systems but
they are redundant
Due to the considerable uncertainty of the naive set-theoretic concepts of
that time (absence of the axiom of extensionality, theories of non-founded sets,
notions of compatibility and independence of set-theoretic axioms, etc.) and the
absence of formal first-order logical theory (predicate calculus, models, and in-
terpretations), Russell’s correct conjectures and intuitions can only be marveled
at.
Consider now Russell’s paradoxical class in formal set theory NBG−, in
which X ∈X and X /∈X are properly formed formulas (pff), and the class
R
def
= {X| X /∈X & X ∈V−} exists because the formula X /∈X is predicative,
49


## Page 50


and, by Proposition 4. 4 (Theorem on the existence of classes) in [27], p. 238,
we obtain that the class of sets R does exist; obviously, R ⊂V−. But Russell’s
paradox R ∈R ⇔R /∈R in the theory of NBG−is not derivable [27], p. 246,
but proves only that R is not a set but a proper class, in contrast to Russell’s
general paradox w ∈w ⇔w /∈w, which showed in the terminology of his time
that w does not exist because it is inconsistent, and in modern terminology
that w is not a universal set, i.e., containing all sets X such that X /∈X. It is
important for us to note here only that even in axiomatic set theory Russell’s
paradox formally involves the formula R ∈R of the reflexive set (in the premises
and conclusions), which turns out to be a proper class and, hence, the formula
R ∈R is not legal and hence the relation R ∈R is simply a fiction since it
is not provided for in NBG−theory, i.e., it is not a pff, as well as the formula
R /∈R.
In all the conclusions of these paradoxes, in addition to the problem of the
existence of these universal objects, there is the same truth-functional operation
of succession (implication), which is false, for example, in Russell’s paradox: If
R
def
= {X| X /∈X} is a set, then from the premise R /∈R follows, by definition
of R, the statement R ∈R. But Proposition 8 shows us that no predicate,
no definition (in this case the universality of the set R) entails the statement
R ∈R, since the latter relation is, in general, indefinable and multivalued
with respect to the reflexive set R. Moreover, even if such unambiguity can be
postulated, for example by Axcel’s anti-founded axiom, the above implication
continues to be false, since if we consider the set M to the set of all founded sets
(Mirimanoff’s paradox), then the premise M /∈M is true, and the conclusion as
the formula M ∈M ⊆V is false. This is what Proposition 8 shows: any solution
X of the equation (41) X = {X0, X1, ..., Xλ, ..., X} where {X0, X1, ..., Xλ, ..., }
the set consisting of the founded sets is a non-founded set, so it cannot belong
to the system of all founded sets. In fact, a reflexive set X is never the result
of putting together non-reflexive sets.
Note also that reflexivity (formal, not real) in Russell’s paradox, as in other
set-theoretic paradoxes, arises also in the formal theory of the first order itself.
In a formal theory (a logistic system, or uninterpreted system, see, e.g., [11],
Introduction, § 07) the atomic formula X ∈X is a “properly formed formula“
(pff) (well-formed formula). One of the inference rules in formal logistic systems,
namely is the substitution rule, leads to a “well-formed reflexive formula“.

introduce the sign S | to indicate the substitution operation, so that Sb

Let’s verify now that the usual argument for Russell’s paradox does not hold
in NBG. By the class existence theorem, there is a class Y = {x | x /∈x}. Then
(∀x)(x ∈Y ⇔x /∈x). In unabbreviated notation this becomes (∀X)(M(X) ⇒
(X ∈Y ⇔X /∈X)). Assume M(Y ). Then Y ∈Y ⇔Y /∈Y , which, by
the tautology (A ⇔¬A) ⇒(A & ¬A), yields Y ∈Y & Y /∈Y . Hence, by the
derived rule of proof by contradiction, we obtain ¬M(Y ). Thus, in NBG−, the
argument for Russell’s paradox merely shows that Russell’s class Y is a proper
class, not a set. NBG−will avoid the paradoxes of Cantor and Burali-Forti in
a similar way. (See this literal proof in [27], p. 246.)
However, due to the lack of interpretations, i.e., semantics, in the logical
system, a correctly formed sub-formula R ∈R is indefinable or, similarly, is non-
well-defined, and the implication R /∈R ⇒R ∈R is false, as opposed to the
true implication R ∈R ⇒R /∈R. In spite of all this, the proof of “multiplicity
of the class R by means of “of Russell’s paradox is correct but redundant: only
one true implication R ∈R ⇒R /∈R is sufficient to circumvent this paradox.
This workaround is applicable to all set-theoretic paradoxes and will be shown
below. Moreover, it gives other, stronger possibilities for proving other, different
results (“is not a set, but a proper class“).
This is justified also by the fact that the formal attraction of Russell’s para-
dox to the proof of “multiplicity“ of some classes is superfluous, and sometimes
simply ridiculous. For example, this can be seen in the so-called relative (or
parameterized) Russell paradox. If for any subclass a ⊆V−we denote the class
a∩R by Ra, then the correctly formed formula (x ∈Ra ⇔x ∈a & x /∈x) after
substituting into it instead of x the set Ra, assuming of course that Ra ∈a,
turns into Russell’s paradoxical relative formula: (Ra ∈Ra ⇔Ra /∈Ra), which
proves such an assumption wrong.
(Note that the assumption M(R) leading to Russell’s paradox is nothing but
the assumption R ∈V−. Since, assuming a = V−, we get R = Ra. Indeed,
Ra = RV−
def
= V−∩R = R and the assumption RV−∈a is nothing but
R ∈V−).
In particular, in the theory ZF −, which considers only sets, the non-existence
of the universal set is deduced by this method; Zermelo showed by the same
reasoning that his set M, containing as its elements each of its subsets, does not
exist; Mirimanoff showed that the set of all ordinary sets (i.e., founded sets) is
inconsistent and thus does not exist.

Formal proofs in logistic systems were invented precisely in order to follow
the condition of rigor, i.e., to ensure that the proof of a theorem does not
use any interpretation, but proceeds only according to the rules of the logistic
system (see [11], § 07). “The purpose of this formalism is, – wrote Church,
– that a proof satisfying our condition of rigor should remain valid under any
interpretation of the logistic system, so that we obtain in the end an economy
by proving different things by a single reasoning. The size of the economy is
determined by the fact that there is no need to repeat an indefinable number
52


## Page 53


of times proofs which coincide in form but differ in content, so how can they be
held all at once, once and for all“ (see [11], Introduction, § 07). Perhaps the
most illustrative of this formalism are the following examples.
Let’s consider the cases when in (46) E = (∼) or E = (¬ ∼), in particular,

as E = (⊆) or E = (¬ ⊆), E = (⊇) or E = (¬ ⊇) and apply a formal method like
the substitution rule to prove the formula (46). We can do it but with great care
because in mathematics, a variable is a symbol containing the name of which
coincides with the content of the proper name, or constant, except that the
single denotation of the constant is replaced here by the possibility of different
variable values. Since it is usually necessary to limit the values that a variable
can take we will assume that with a variable y a nonempty range of its possible
values is associated with it. Therefore, the content of a variable includes, in a
sense, the content of the proper name of its meaning area. And if we consider

E = (⊆), E = (⊇) in NBG, then a region of the forms ∃x∀y[yEx ⇔¬(yEy)]
with a variable y is empty. Thus Substitution rule does not work but formally
under the assumption ∃x∀y[yEx ⇔¬(yEy)] we can obtain [xEx ⇔¬(xEx)].

proving that such x does not exist what was clear from the beginning in (46)

fake paradox [¬(x = x) ⇔(x = x)], which proves in NBG−theory that ¬M(x),

If we consider E as ≤Card or ≥Card, then the corresponding propositional

Card(y))] with a variable y and a constant x have an empty regions. Thus in
these cases the substitution rule does not work at all.
We can speak about serious attitude and correct understanding of the reflex-

Zermelo posed the following question: “Does there exist a set X such that
each subset of it is an element of the set X?“His own answer was “No“ [39].
Moreover, he proved it by using a paradoxical set like in above examples. He
supposed that such set X existed and consider the following subspace Z

exist.
Here we also exclude, although logically permissible, but from our point of

which leads either to the non-existence of the set X, or to the proof that X is
