3. Mirimanoff-type paradoxes.
This is also the basis of Mirimanoff’s proof (the paradox of his name) that
the set of all founded sets (note – outside any formal theory) does not exist: if it
exists, it is a founded set, since otherwise it would have an non-founded element
(true statement); if it is a founded set, it is an element of itself (false statement,
since the hypothesis of the existence of a universal class of founded sets has not
yet been proved, but is put forward as a hypothesis, and an incorrect one at
that), hence it is an non-founded set. See [29], p. 43. In the formal theory
of NBG or NBG−, where such a universal class of founded sets exists as a
consequence of the existence axioms for classes, Mirimanoff’s paradox proves
only that V is not a set, but is a proper class. Although even here the logistic
formalism is redundant.
Mirimanoff’s paradox is solved in the same way: the family of all of ordinal
(in modern terminology – founded) sets does not exist [29], p. 43. Its proof
reduces to the proof of the non-existence of the family all of sets of the first
kind (in modern terminology – non-reflexive sets) [29], p. 40, since the latter
set is a subset of the former. A curious proof of Mirimanoff’s paradox is given
later in [37] and [29], p. 15: let M be a family of all founded sets. If the family
M exists, then it is founded (true statement); indeed, otherwise there would be
an infinite sequence of ∈-relations M ∋X1 ∋X2 ∋X3 ∋...and hence there
would be an infinite sequence of ∈-relations X1 ∋X2 ∋X3 ∋...., which would
mean that the set X1 ∈M is non-founded, which contradicts the fact that
all elements of the family M are founded sets; now, if it is proved that M is a
founded set, and it is itself a family of all founded sets, then it contains itself, i.e.
M ∈M, hence it is non-founded. By Mirimanoff’s non-existence, for subsequent
authors the assumption that M is a set is incorrect, so this set M is a proper
class. There is an incorrect implication in this proof: from the universality of
the family M and the correct proof that M is a founded family or presumably
a set, it does not follow that M ∈M, since reflexive sets (classes, hyper-classes,
etc., if they are admissible in certain axiom systems) are not obtained by simply
attaching a set to itself, as can be seen from the experience of non-founded set
theory. Like the case of Russell’s paradox, Mirimanoff’s paradox is solved as
follows.
Let’s denote by M the class of all founded sets in NBG−, which we will call
the class of all ordinal Mirimanoff sets, and by N the class of all non-founded
sets in NBG−, which we will call the class of all extraordinary Mirimanoff sets.

NBG, which in exactly this theory NBG is defined by the predicative formula
X /∈X and obviously M ⊂V−. The class N also exists by the aforementioned
Axiom of Additivity in NBG−theory.

4. Cantor-type paradoxes
Note here that Cantor’s classical “diagonal method“ ([9], p. 75-78) later
acquired a formal form similar in form to the Russell’s paradox. Indeed, if X is
an arbitrary set, and P(X) is the set of all its subsets, then Cantor’s theorem
states that there is no bijection between the sets X and P(X). Indeed, if such a
bijection f : X →P(X) existed, then the subset K
def
= {x ∈X| x /∈f(x)} would
be contradictory: the element x0 = f −1(K) would satisfy the following formula:
x0 ∈K ⇔x0 /∈K (indeed, the formula is similar to Russell’s paradoxical
formula: R ∈R ⇔R /∈R). Therefore, there is no such element x0 in X, but
then there is no correspondence to the subset K of the set X in X, which means
there is no bijection of f.
The same proof is available in a more general formulation of the theorem
(see, for example, [22], Chap. V, §3.), from which Cantor’s theorem follows.:
If the domain of definition T of an arbitrary function f is contained in X, the
values of which are subsets of the set X, then the set K
def
= {x ∈T| x /∈f(x)}
is not the value of the function f, or in other words, K /∈f(T).
Indeed, if for some x0 ∈X we have the formula f(x0) = K, or in another
way, K ∈f(T), then we get the same contradiction: x0 ∈K ⇔x0 /∈K, which
proves the statement of the theorem.
As in the case of the non-elimination
of Russell’s paradox in the formal theory of NBG−, the evidence in various
specific cases is redundant and ridiculous, obscuring the essence of the matter.
For example, if T = X, and f is an injective mapping that maps to each element
of x ∈X a one-element subset of {x} ⊂X, or, equivalently, {x} ∈P(X),
then it is obvious that in this particular case K = ∅and it is obvious that
∅/∈{{x}| x ∈X} = f(X). Although from the general formal proof we get
a contradictory formula: x0 ∈∅⇔xo /∈∅, which is really contradictory, but
trivially ridiculous, since the formula x0 ∈∅is incorrect, and the formula x0 /∈∅
is correct.
The second extreme. If T = X, and f is a constant map whose value is an
empty subset of the set X, then it is obvious that in this case K = X and that
60


## Page 61


the formula f(x0) = X does not work out for any x0 ∈X, or, which is the same
as X /∈f(X). Although the same is obtained from a formal proof through a
contradictory formula: x0 ∈X ⇔xo /∈X, which is really contradictory, but
trivially ridiculous, since the formula x0 ∈X is correct, and the formula x0 /∈X
is incorrect.
Meanwhile, the above Cantor’s Theorem can be proved by bypassing the
above-mentioned contradictory formulas. Indeed, the set is K ∈f(T). Then
there is an element of t ∈T such that f(t) = K.
By defining the set K,
we obtain the correct relation K /∈K. Then, according to the Pair Axiom,
there exists a one-element set {K}, according to the Sum Axiom, there exists
K′ = K ⊔{K}, which contains the set K as its proper subset, which contradicts
the maximality of the set K. Therefore, K /∈f(T). □
5. Hilbert-type paradoxes
Hilbert’s paradox is solved in a similar way, which he never published, be-
cause, according to him, this paradox was known to all experts in set theory,
especially Cantor ([41], p. 204). Let’s present it according to the source [40], p.
505-506. The paradox is based on the special concept of a set, given by Hilbert
on the basis of two principles.
The first principle of additivity, which says that any two sets can be combined
into one set as well as combine infinitely many sets into one set that contains
elements of all these sets. The second principle is the mapping principle, which
says, that for any set M there exists a set MM of all mappings of the same
into itself. Then he constructs the universe U of all sets obtained by applying
these two principles indefinitely to the set N of all natural numbers. Finally,
he gets the last set F = UU and concludes “that since F is formed by only
two principles, it is obtained only from natural numbers“, he concludes, what is
F ∈U. (By the way, the first principle instantly implies inclusion F ⊆U, since
the union of two sets F ∪U contains elements of each of them.) Considering
“that there are no more elements in the set F than in the set U“, Hilbert
supposed that “there is a bijection between U and F that corresponds to each
ui ∈U a map fi ∈F and, by Cantor’s diagonal method, constructed such a
mapping g : U →U such that g /∈F, what is in contradiction with the fact that
F is the set of all maps from U to U“.
(The assumption of a bijection between F and U follows from the Cantor-
Berstein theorem, since there is already one bijection F →U, by consequence
of F ⊆F from the previous predicate F ∈U and the second U →F, which can
easily be solved by comparing each element u ∈U to a constant map U →U,
the image of which coincides with u.)
Hilbert concluded from this “that UU is always greater than U, but according
to the construction UU ⊂U what is a contradiction .
But it could have been simpler: to show the same diagonal Cantor’s method.,
that for any set M, the set MM is not a subset of the set M, and therefore
M is a proper subset of M ∪{M}. This instantly implies that there is no
universal set in the Hilbert sense. At the same time, Hilbert’s uncertainty does
not matter, as “combining sets an infinite number of times into one set“: no
matter how many times you combine all constructed sets into a universal set U,
61


## Page 62


the set UU always expands the set U.
Without any antinomy, this Hilbert method shows that there are no universal
sets, there are no universal sets in the understanding of classical formal theories
ZFC and NBG−, there are no universal classes for those formal theories that
allow their own classes to be elements of hyper classes, etc.
6. Propositions’ paradox
Let’s recall the following “Paradox of Propositions“ [41], 2.3). Let m be a
set of statements, and Q m be a statement such that “every statement m ∈m is
true“ (considered as a possible infinite conjunction of statements). Then, if m
and n are distinct sets, then the statements Q m and Q n are also distinct, and
the mapping which for every statement m associates the statement Q m is an
injection. Therefore, if we consider the set P = {p | ∃m(Q m = p & p /∈m)},
by virtue of the above-mentioned injectivity, we obtain a contradiction. Indeed,
Q P ∈P ⇔Q P /∈P.
In the propositional paradox, the family of propositions P is not a set.
Indeed, let’s assume the opposite, and the family P of all such statements
{p | ∃m(Q m = p & p /∈m)} is a set. Let Q P be a one-element set consisting
of a single statement P. Then, by definition Q{P}, this is a statement that
is true, since the only utterance of the set {P} consists of true statements of
p, which, by definition, are equal to Q m for some set m of true statements of
m. But, obviously, that {P} does not combine with P, because of the same
injection, since P ̸= {Q P}. Let’s now consider the union of sets of statements
P∪{Q P}, which is a set and contains its proper subset P, since we have already
shown that P is P, which contradicts the maximality of the set P. Therefore,
P is not a set, but is a proper class, for which the concept of class conjunction
Q P is not applicable
6. There are essentially no universal sets for most predicates:
¬∃X(∀x)(x ∈X) vs ∃X ⊢¬{X}
In the previous two paragraphs, we have shown that the well-known set-
theoretic paradoxes are imaginary, i.e., they are really not contradictions, and
have circumvented them using the maximality principle method. An impor-
tant consequence of this approach is that the reality, generally speaking, is the
following: “There is no universal set containing all sets with some property“,
e.g, X = {x | x /∈x} . More generally, “For any class of elements, there is an
element that does not belong to this class“, in other words, any class of ele-
ments is incomplete. This of course contradicts the first axiom of the existence
of the class of all pairs of sets (a, b) for which a ∈b in NBG-theory. This axiom
prohibits proper classes from being elements of other classes, for example, {V},
but this does not mean that in fact there is no such pair as {{V}, {V, {V}},
when a = V and b = {V}. Formal theories allowing for this exist and are
consistent: 7.5 Classes Taken Seriously – the System of Quine and Morse; 7.6.
Classes not Taken Seriously – Systems of Bernays and Quine; 7.7. The System
of Ackermann. See [18], p. 138-153; see also [12], Chap. II, §7.
In this case, we conclude that the very statement, for example, by Russell:
“The class of all non-reflexive classes is contradictory“, is incorrect, but it is
62


## Page 63


true that “the class of all non-reflexive sets does not exist, more precisely, it
is indefinable“, and according to Cantor’s intuition, “it is not a completed or
final thing“. One can find another names for such thing as “trans-definite sets
without boundary“ see [5], p. 311. Paul Cohen expressed the intuition of this
indeterminacy of the eternal process of forming new sets or numbers (see below
about Conway numbers): “Perhaps the main reason for accepting the axiom of
infinity is the absurdity of the idea that adding only one set at a time could
exhaust the entire world“. (See [12], Chap. IV, §13. Conclusion.)
This does not mean at all that there are no universal classes in mathematics,
in particular, in set theory. On the contrary, they exist and there are quite a
lot of them. For example, the set of all natural numbers. In a strict sense, the
existence of this set is determined by the axiom of infinity. The “incompleteness
or non-final things“, mentioned above, differ from the set of all natural numbers
because they cannot be defined even axiomatically, since any such definition
will make them complete or final, which is easily proved by the Russell functor:
“The subclass of all non-reflexive objects of this universal set is not contained
in the universe“, which contradicts a possible axiom: “axiomatic existence of
the universe“.
Such universe is strikingly different from the second example, namely, the
existence of the set of all countable ordinals. Otherwise, if the set of all countable
ordinals is not countable, then it itself, as an ordered set, defines the cardinal
that is larger than all cardinals included in it, and the set consisting of these
ordinals and the last one will obviously be countable, which contradicts the
universality of the assumed set of all countable ordinals. Thus, our assumption
about its countability is false.
These “incompleteness or non-final things“ are not mathematical objects
because they can be compared and called as unfinished processes (“eternal pro-
cesses“, perpetuum mobile processes sequences, etc.) that are continuing and
cannot end, by definition. Indeed, for any set X we imply Russell’s functor
FR(X)
def
= X ∪{{x | x ∈X & x /∈x}} and obtain a new set X1 = FR(X) ⊃X.
Then continue this process for each non-limit ordinal and for a limit ordinal α
we consider Xα =
S
α′<α
Xα′ and put Xα+1 = FR(Xα), and so on, and forever.
Of course it is only an idea because there is no final object only tendency.
The Russell functor is nothing more than a counting functor, or a construc-
tion functor for a class of ordinal numbers. Indeed, let’s set X = ∅and obtain
the class of all ordinals according to Mirimanoff and von Neumann:
0 = ∅, 1 = {∅}, 2 = {∅, {∅}}, ..., ω = {0, 1, 2, ...}, ω = {0, 1, 2, ..., ω + 1}, ...
for 0 ≤α < Ωbut actually we can continue this process Ω, Ω+ 1, ..., Ω+ ω, ...
and go on forever, obtaining an indefinable object of arbitrary ordinal numbers
α ≥0, or by Ulrich Blau, “trans-definite ordinals without boundary“[5].
Since each ordinal number is determined by a set of previously defined ordi-
nal numbers, the statement about the possibility of the existence of a set of all
ordinal numbers is incorrect. The Burali-Forti paradox consisted precisely in
this incorrect assumption. And the following reality or situation is true: “The
63


## Page 64


ordinal number formation functor through all the previous ones (Cantor, Mi-
rimanoff, von Neumann, Conway, Blau, etc.) starts the following well-ordered
counting process: FOrd(α + 1) = [0, α) ∪{α}“.
And this process continues
without completion or finality, let’s say that it is a “limitlessness“.
Considering that too extensive sets lead to contradictions, von Neumann
in 1925 proposed his formal set theory, which excluded such extensive sets,
requiring that they should not be elements of either the sets themselves or
other equally extensive classes, i.e., in the terminology of Bernays and G¨odel,
proper classes.
However, like the previous formal system of Zermelo Fraenkel (ZFC) with
Choice Axiom, which excluded such vast sets altogether, the NBG system pro-
ceeded from the same intuition and the premise of the presence (not formalized
in any way) of some well-defined nonempty class (objects), the so-called uni-
verse of reasoning, or the world under consideration (universe of discourse) is
in the ZFC system, and some class in which subsets are identified with their
characteristic functions is in NBG, and in the latter case, functions that are
element sets, i.e. arguments, and functions that are not arguments and not
elements, but proper classes. And both assumed classes are static and do not
contain any elements, sets, or classes outside of themselves. But in reality this
is not the case, and such restrictions have no advantage other than some formal
convenience and the imaginary elimination of set-theoretic antinomies.
Note that in an unfinished or non-final thing, in the sense of Cantor, there is
no characteristic function for this thing, since it itself is incomplete or non-final.
A set is a collection of definite, distinguishable objects of perception or
thought which is perceived as a whole.
The objects are known as elements
or members of a set.
Following Cantor: “Namely, multiplicity may have the property that the
perception of the ‘co-existence‘ of all its elements leads to a contradiction, so
that this multiplicity cannot be considered as a unity, as ‘some kind of complete
thing‘. I call such multiplicities absolutely infinite or inconsistent multiplicities.
As it is easy to see, ‘the totality of everything imaginable‘, for example, it is
a similar multiplicity; other examples will appear later. On the contrary, if a
set of elements of a certain multiplicity can be thought of without contradiction
as a set of ‘co-existing‘ elements, so that they can be combined into a ‘single
thing‘, then I call it a consistent set or ‘set‘ (in French and Italian, this concept
is appropriately expressed by the words ‘ensemble‘ and ‘insieme‘ “) [10].
So, in NBG−-theory, there are objects that are not elements of any sets
or classes, but are proper classes of V−. We will show that these objects can
be subjected, for example, Conway numbers No to algebraic operations similar
to operations on proper classes: intersection of X ⊂V−and Y ⊂V−, i.e.,
X ∩Y ⊂V−, union of X and Y , i.e., X ∪Y ⊂V−, difference of X and Y ,
i.e., X \ Y ⊂V−. Although X and Y cannot be elements, they as do other
similar proper classes of V−can be consider as an algebraic structure of a
linearly ordered field similar to Boolean algebra for subsets. Thus, the formula
⊢Pr(X) ⇒¬∃{X}, i.e., non-existence of the singleton of X, that prohibits

proper classes from being elements does not prevent these proper classes X ⊆
No from having an algebraic and topological structure. Thus, by introducing
the concept of super-classes, we obtain the usual form of a topological linearly
ordered field, but for the sake of argument, this can be done in the language
of proper classes, although it is not as convenient as introducing super-classes.
Let us do this carefully.
For this purpose we consider a linear ordering topology on No, i.e., a class
X ∈No is open in a linear ordering topology on No, if for every x ∈X there
exists a positive number ε ∈No such that the interval (x −ε, x + ε) ⊂X.

68


## Page 69


In spite of the fact that the formal logical system NBG−is convenient in
many cases (e.g., Conway theory of numbers and games, as well as Category
Theory and many other mathematical theories and constructions) by the finite-
ness of its axioms, by existence of universal objects (proper classes) identified
with “properties“ or “singular proposition functions“, etc., there is a lack in it
which, by thought of P. J. Cohen, the “theory NBG−is a less intuitive system
than ZF“([12], Cpt. 3, §5). Indeed, such formal systems are very formal and
they were inputed to avoid so-called set-theoretic paradoxes of Russell’s type.
Often these “tricks“ (proofs by contradiction via paradoxes) were out of con-
tents and concepts of mathematical notions and definitions like “reflexive sets“,
“universal objects“, “premises of false propositions“, “undetectable objects“,
etc.
The only correct and meaningful conclusion from Russell’s paradox is the
following: “Any family of non-reflexive elements is always non-reflexive and is
not contained in the original family“. As a consequence of it is the following:
“There is no universal families like all sets, all ordinals, all groups, all rings,
all fields, etc.“, but there are internal processes, perpetuum mobile processes
sequences, ordinal numbers without border, endlessness or something like that.
Part Three
1. Conway numbers as a platform for various mathematical and
other models
As we saw in the previous section, there is no such mathematical object as
the collection of all ordinals although each ordinal exists individually, since it is
determined by all the preceding ordinals the totality of which is a mathematical
object because it is a set or a proper class.
Moreover, if for each ordinal we can enter a symbol, as, for instance, above
α, then for a non-existent “collection“ of all ordinals we cannot formally enter
a symbol, for instance, O and write the formula α ∈O because the right part
of it O does not exist.
On the other hand, since we can consider a similar eternal process of ordinal
numbers of the form ξ = ωων, ν ≥0, we obtain the corresponding process of
increasing linearly ordered fields of Conway numbers Rζ ⊂Rζ′, where ζ′ =
ωων+1, ν ≥0, and then for any ordinal α we can write the correct formula
α ∈Rζ for some fixed and even smallest ordinal number ν ≥0, since both α
and Rζ are determined by their existing as mathematical objects.
Thus, a linearly ordered field of Conway numbers Rζ of the highest power
can be a platform for models of mathematical formal systems.

2. The difference between infinity and an infinite number in the
structure of the Conway numbers Field
The concept of infinity is well known to everyone from the school curriculum,
and the symbol of infinity ∞has firmly entered the universal vocabulary.
Recall that in the field of real numbers R, a sequence (xn)0≤n<ω is called
infinitely large if for any positive number E ∈R there exists a natural number
n0 such that |xn| > E for all n > n0.
At the same time, the so-called infinities with a sign are distinguished, i.e.,
+∞and −∞, i.e., a sequence (xn)0≤n<ω is called infinitely large with a sign
+ if for any positive number E ∈R there exists a natural number n0 such
that xn > E for all n > n0 and a sequence (xn)0≤n<ω is called infinitely large
with a sign −if for any positive number E ∈R there exists a natural number
n0 such that xn < −E for all n > n0 . (Let’s mention also dual notions of
sequences (xn)0≤n<ω which are called infinitely small, i.e., if for any positive
number ε ∈R there exists a natural number n0 such that |xn| < ε for all
n > n0 and also with sign as for sequences ( 1
n)0<n<ω and (−1

Symbols of infinity +∞and −∞became convenient abbreviations: (−∞<
x < +∞), (−∞< x < b) and (a < x < +∞) as infinite intervals of all
real numbers x between these symbols and numbers and studying the behavior
of functions at infinity (asymptotic behavior, classification or order of infinitely
large quantities, etc.). However, there was no understanding of the fundamental
difference between symbols of infinity and the infinite numbers that appeared
in Cantor’s set theory (ordinal and cardinal numbers). Nevertheless, there is
a fundamental difference between them, there are heterogeneous concepts –
numbers and symbols of infinity.

Moreover, “these gaps definable as upper or lower bounds are particularly

In fact, the Conway’s symbols above are by breed nature transdefinite num-
bers and they are no different from definitite numbers – they are mathematical
entities that can be added, multiplied, subtracted, and divided (not by zero, of
course) like any Conway number, using identical formulas, what cannot be done
with symbols of infinity +∞and −∞, except of course primitive operations
with them sometimes (in Mathematical Calculus or in Measure Theory), e.g.,
∞+ ∞= ∞, x · ∞= ∞, for any positive number x, but ∞−∞and 0 · ∞are
undefined.
We also recall from the course of mathematical analysis that every upper
bounded subset X of real numbers R has an exact upper bound sup X ∈R,
i.e., the smallest upper bound; and every lower bounded subset X ⊆R has an
exact lower bound inf X ∈R, i.e., the greatest lower bound (a fact equivalent to
Dedekind’s Theorem). For a set X that is unbounded from above, it is assumed
sup X = +∞, and for a set X that is unbounded from below, it is assumed
sup X = −∞. In particular, for the empty subset ∅of the set of real numbers,
we obtain sup ∅= −∞and inf ∅= +∞.
There is a completely different nature of subsets and proper subclasses X ⊆
No of the collection No of all Conway numbers. If a set X ⊂No does not
have a maximum element, then there is no exact upper bound sup X in No;
and if a set X ⊂No does not have a minimum element in No, then there is no
exact lower bound inf X in No. Conway posited, as we saw above sup No =
{No | } = +Ωas the end of the number line (or the last term Ωin our notation
here) as the Dedekind section (No, ∅), which is a gap and an illegal object and
called it improper class and speak of there being an impropriety of gap (see
[14], p. 38) (we can add naturally the Dedekind section (∅, No), which is a
gap and also an illegal object and put { | No} = −Ω), but Conway did not
define a natural logically possible particular degenerate example sup ∅and inf ∅,
respectively. The latter can also be defined in NBG theory as sup ∅= −∞Ω

Indeed, if a set X ⊂No does not have a maximum element in No, then
consider a proper class Y = {y | y > x (∀x) x ∈X}, then Y does not have a
smallest element y0. Otherwise, y1 = {X | y0} ∈No has the following property:
y1 > x (∀x) x ∈X and y1 < y0 and hence y1 /∈Y . Contradiction. And if a
set X ⊂No does not have a minimal element, then consider a proper class
Y = {y | y < x (∀x) x ∈X}, then Y does not have a smallest element y0.
Otherwise, y1 = {y | X} ∈No has the following property: y1 < x (∀x) x ∈X
and y1 > y0 and hence y1 /∈Y . Contradiction.
The existence of trans-definite Conway numbers (e.g., formal systems with
super-classes) allows us to define the exact upper and exact lower bounds of sets
or proper classes of trans-definite numbers such that do not have maximum and
minimum numbers, respectively.
Indeed, if there is any set X or proper class of Conway numbers, or any col-
lection of trans-definite numbers obtained by Conway’s formulas and extending
beyond the class of all Conway numbers such that it does not have maximum
or minimum element, it has the exact upper bound and exact lower bound, re-
spectively, but these bounds are not Conway numbers at all in either the NBG
theory or the theory that extends beyond NBG but symbols, i.e., the sym-
bols that separate X and any numerical upper bounds and any numerical lower
bounds, respectively. These symbols are like “immaterial“ boundaries, if we
assume that Conway numbers are ideal material points or “material entities“,
figuratively we can compare them our earthly understanding of boundaries on
the sea or ocean, in contrast to the visible boundaries on the earth. Or, in an-
other way, if we consider the geometric interpretation of Conway numbers and
trans-definite Conway numbers as points on a line, then the symbols of infinity
are not points at all, but rather conditional separators of points on this line.
These symbols can also be considered as punctuation marks that are part of the
syntax of formal systems.

3. The beginning of measure theory for closed and open intervals of
Conway numbers
Now we will look at the natural geometry of Conway numbers, i.e., con-
sider these numbers in an obvious metric ρ(a, b) = |b −a| for every pair of
Conway numbers a and b. It is clear that ρ(a, a) = 0, ρ(a, b) = ρ(b, a) and
ρ(a, b) + ρ(b, c) ≥ρ(a, c), for any Conway numbers a, b, c. Thus we obtain the
corresponding metric topology or lineary topology with a base {(a, b)}, where
a, b run over “all“ Conway numbers (more precisely, for all a, b ∈No in NBG
theory and for each numbers a, b in super-class theories or in the “eternal pro-
cess theory“). The only difference of usual metric and topology in the case Rω
and the other cases Rζ, ζ = ωων, ν > 0, is that in the case ν = 0 we have
Archimedean field, i.e., for all numbers x, y ∈Rω such that 0 < x < y there
is a natural number n > 0 such that n · x > y, and for ν > 0 the fields Rζ
are not Archimedean in above sense, i.e., there is no n such that n · x > y for
all numbers x, y ∈Rζ, ν > 0, such that 0 < x < y, but ζ-Archimedean fields,
i.e., for all numbers x, y ∈Rζ, ν > 0, such that 0 < x < y there is an ordinal
α ∈(0, ζ) such that α · x > y (see details in [25]). Of course in eternal processes
the field of the corresponding case is not ζ-Archimedean at all for any ζ = ωων,
83


## Page 84


ν ≥0, but nevertheless, for any pair of numbers such that 0 < x < y there is
an ordinal α such that α · x > y.
Despite the fact that the latter case excludes the existence of such a field
as non-mathematical, it can be mentally contemplated or viewed as Conway
did (see [14], p. 37, 42) on the straight “number line“ consisting of Conway
numbers or the “number plane“ in the case of imaginary Conway numbers
Cξ = Rξ + iRξ, ξ = ωων, ν ≥0.
Next, for any two different Conway numbers a and b, we can determine the
length of the segments [a, b], [a, b), (a, b] and (a, b) as a Conway number ρ(a, b)
in any fixed linear orded field Rζ, ζ = ωων, 0 ≤ν ≤Ωin NBG theory as well
as in eternal processes, i.e., in cases of super-classes.
In the same sense, we cannot determine the length of the intervals, say
(−∞−ω, +∞ω), (−∞−ω, b) and (a, +∞ω), etc. because as we saw above there
is a fundamental difference between numbers and symbols of infinity and thus
there are no numbers that express the “length“ of these intervals because there
is no numbers (like +∞ω and −∞−ω and thus +∞ω −∞−ω, b −∞−ω and
+∞ω −a) that correspond to what we would call the “length“ of an interval,
say (−∞−ω, +∞ω), (−∞−ω, b) and (a, +∞ω), etc. that contains all numbers
x ∈No (or x ∈Rζ) such that −∞−ω < x < +∞ω, −∞−ω < x < b and
a < x < +∞ω, respectively, etc. as well as in eternal processes, i.e., say “each
super Conway number x“.
Nevertheless, we can compare such intervals as geometric objects by isomet-
ric embeddings one into another, i.e., for example, for intervals (+∞a, b] and
(+∞a′, b′], if the is an isometric mapping i : (+∞a, b] →(+∞a′, b′], i.e., for all
numbers x, y ∈(+∞a, b] we have ρ(i(x), i(y)) = ρ(x, y), then we say that the
“length“ of (+∞a, b] (more exactly, the “space“ of all Conway numbers in it
in NBG theory, i.e., the proper class (+∞a, b] ∩No) is less than or equal to
the “length“ of (+∞a′, b′]; if i is an isometric mapping onto (+∞a′, b′], then
the “length“ of (+∞a, b] and (+∞a′, b′] are equal; if (+∞a′, b′] is not equal to
(+∞a, b] then we say sorter that (+∞a′, b′] is greater than (+∞a, b] or what is
the same (+∞a, b] is less than (+∞a′, b′].
Note that for every Rξ, ξ = ωων, ν ≥0, the definitions are similar.
For example, for any Conway number a ∈[0, +∞ω) the “length“ of [0, +∞ω)
and [a, +∞ω) are equal because of the isometric mapping i(x) = x + a.
On the other hand, we will see that the “length“ of an interval (+∞ω, b],
for any +∞ω < b < +∞ω
2 , is less that the “length“ of (+∞ω, ω] but not equal
to it as opposed to intervals (+∞ω, b], for any +∞ω
2 < b < ω which are equal
to (+∞ω, ω].

ciprocals ( 1

periodic “compressions“.

We will show now that the λ-sequences (α)0≤α<λ of ordinal numbers 0 ≤
α < λ for different limit ordinals λ ̸= λ′ have usually different or equal “jumps“,
but especially periodic with their own laws which we are going to study. We
also add that these laws cannot be revealed from the classical representation
of ordinal numbers, as types of well-ordered sets. For clarity, we will set some
simple questions and problems, concerning “jumps“.
Question 1. The “length“ of [0, +∞λ) is less/greater than the “length“ of
(+∞λ, λ] or it is equal to the “length“ of (+∞λ, λ]?
We will see that the second part of the question can be solved by one word:
“Never“; the first part of the question can be solved like this: “Can be less
and can be greater“, e.g., the “length“ of [0, +∞ω) is less than the “length“ of
(+∞ω, ω]; the “length“ of [0, +∞2·ω) is greater than the “length“ of (+∞2·ω, 2·
ω].
Question 2. Can “lengths“ of (+∞λ, λ] and (+∞λ′, λ′] for different λ, λ′
be equal?
We will see that the second question can be solved like this: “Yes and no“,
e.g., the “length“ of (+∞ω2, ω2] is greater than the “length“ of (+∞ω, ω]; and
the “length“ of (+∞2·ω, 2 · ω] is equal to the “length“ of (+∞ω, ω].
Question 3. What are the necessary and sufficient conditions for a positive
answer to the first part of the first question?
Question 4. Can the “jumps“ of canonical sequences be arbitrarily large?
The fourth question can be solved like this: ”Yes. And it can be “unimaginably
huge“.
Question 5. Can the “length“ of [0, +∞λ) be equal to the “length“ of
[α′, +∞λ) for each ordinal 0 < α′ < λ?
The answer is this: “Can be and can be not“, e.g., the “length“ of [0, +∞ω)
is equal to the “length“ of [n, +∞ω), 0 < n < ω; the “length“ of [0, +∞2·ω) is
not equal to the “length“ of [ω, +∞2·ω).
Proposition 15. Let (α)0≤α<λ be a λ-sequence with limit ordinal λ. Then
the isometric mapping i : [0, +∞λ) →No, given by formula i(x) = λ −x,
maps [0, +∞λ) into (+∞λ, λ] ⊂No if and only if for each pair of ordinals
0 ≤α, α′ < λ, there is an inequality α + α′ < λ.
Proof of “if“.
If for each pair of ordinals 0 ≤α, α′ < λ, there is an
inequality α+α′ < λ, then for each x ∈[0, +∞λ) there is an ordinal 0 ≤α′ < λ
such that x ≤α′ and we have i(x) ≥i(α′), i.e., λ −x ≥λ −α′. But α < λ −α′
for all 0 ≤α < λ because of condition α + α′ < λ for all ordinals 0 ≤α, α′ < λ.
Thus, by the above inequality λ −x ≥λ −α′, we obtain α < λ −x for all
ordinals 0 ≤α, α′ < λ. And we conclude that i(x) ∈(+∞λ, λ] for every number
x ∈[0, +∞λ).
Proof of “only if“. If the isometric mapping i : [0, +∞λ) →No, given by
formula i(x) = λ −x, maps [0, +∞λ) into (+∞λ, λ] ⊂No, then for arbitrary
ordinal α′ ∈[0, +∞λ) we have an inequality λ−α′ > α for all ordinals 0 ≤α < λ
and what means that α + α′ < λ for each pair of ordinals 0 ≤α, α′ < λ. □

µ ≥1.

One can easily see that λ = ωµ for some ordinal µ ≥1. Otherwise, the limit
odinal λ should be of a canonical form λ = ωζ1 · n1 + ωζ2 · n2 + ... + ωζk · nk,
ζ1 > ζ2 > .. > .ζk > 0 and 0 < n1, n2, ..., nk < ω. Clearly, α′ = ωζ2 · n2 + ... +

and λ = ωζ1 · n1, where ζ1 = µ and n1 = 1. If it is not so, i.e., n1 > 1, then put
α = ωζ1 < ωµ and α′ = ωζ1 · (n1 −1) < λ and thus α + α′ = λ. Contradiction.
Therefore, λ = ωµ. □
Proposition 17. Let (α)0≤α<λ be a λ-sequence with limit ordinal λ. Then
the isometric mapping i : [0, +∞λ) →[a, +∞λ) ⊂No, given by formula i(x) =
x + a for any fixed number 0 ≤a ∈[0, +∞λ) maps [0, +∞λ) onto [a, +∞λ) if
and only if λ = ωµ for some ordinal number µ ≥1.
Proof of “if“. If the isometric mapping i : [0, +∞λ) →[a, +∞λ) ⊂No,
given by formula i(x) = x + a for any fixed number 0 ≤x ∈[0, +∞λ) maps
[0, +∞λ) onto [a, +∞λ), then for any ordinals 0 ≤α ≤α′ < λ we obtain
i(α) = α + α′ < λ when a = α′ and x = α. Then, by Proposition 16 (Proof of
“only if“), we obtain λ = ωµ for some ordinal µ ≥1.
Proof of “only if“. If λ = ωµ for some ordinal µ ≥1, then, by Proposition
16 (Proof of “if“), α+α′ < λ for any ordinals 0 ≤α < α′ < λ. Let a ∈[0, +∞λ)
be a fixed number and x be an arbitrary number in [0, +∞ωµ). If we consider
the smallest ordinal α such that a ≤α and the smallest ordinal α′ such that
x ≤α′, then i(x) = x + a ≤α′ + α < ωµ. Thus, i is an injection. For any
number x′ ∈[a, +∞ωµ) we have 0 ≤x′ −a < +∞ωµ. Assuming x = x′ −a we
obtain i(x) = x′. Thus, i is a surjection. □
Proposition 17 is a more general statement than the well-known ordinal
number theory statement that we used above when studying self-similar skands
in Proposition 4, which is the well-known ordinal number theory statement is
the following:
In order for every remainder of α ̸= 0 to be equal to α, it is necessary and
sufficient that α = σ + ρ.
86


## Page 87


And we remind the reader that an ordinal number ρ is called the remainder
of an ordinal α if ρ ̸= 0 and there exists an ordinal number σ such that α = σ+ρ.
In this case, the ordinal numbers introduced by Cantor are considered as
a type of well-ordered sets, in particular, an ordinal α is a type of the well-
ordered set {0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ...}, where 0 ≤α′ < α, ρ is a type of the well-ordered
set {0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ...}, where 0 ≤α′ < ρ and σ is a type of the well-ordered set
{0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ...}, where 0 ≤α′ < σ.
Moreover, σ + ρ is a usual sum of ordinal numbers and differs from sum of
Conway numbers, i.e., σ + ρ above means Cantor’s arithmetic and in general
σ + ρ ̸= ρ + σ while in Conway’s arithmetic always σ + ρ = ρ + σ; σ + ρ ̸< ρ
when σ < ρ in Cantor’s arithmetic and σ + ρ = max{σ + ρ, ρ + σ} in Conway’s
arithmetic.
The approach to ordinal numbers may seem “similar“ to that of Cantor and
Conway, but Conway’s approach is quite different in form Cantor’s approach,
although they are similar in content, and significantly different in the form and
thus in arithmetic.
Indeed, if Conway’s canonical form α = {0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ... | }, where 0 ≤α′ <
α, of an ordinal number α is similar to Cantor’s definition of α as a type of
the well-ordered set {0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ...}, where 0 ≤α′ < α, then Conway’s other
forms of the same number α = {α0, α0 + 1, α0 + 2, ..., α0 + α′, ... | } for every
fixed α0 < α and all α′ such that α0 ≤α0 + α′ < α, do not match Can-
tor’s definition in the same way since, in general, types of the well-ordered sets
{0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ...} and {α0, α0 +1, α0 +2, ..., α0 +α′, ...} are different, in general,
e.g., {0, 1, 2, ..., n−1} is according to Cantor an ordinal n and {0, 1, 2, ..., n−1 | }
is according to Conway an ordinal n, but {2, 3, 4, ..., n −1} is according to Con-
way an ordinal n −2 although {2, 3, 4, ..., n −1 | } is still n. On the other hand,
ω = {0, 1, 2, ..., α, ... | } = {α0, α0 + 1, α0 + 2, ..., α0 + α, ... | }, for every fixed
0 < α0 < ω and both sets {0, 1, 2, ..., α, ...} and {α0, α0 +1, α0 +2, ..., α0 +α, ...}
are of the same type. This is the meaning of the above statement, which we will
prove now in terms of Conway’s number theory.
Note that despite the difference in the form of the definition of ordinal
numbers and their arithmetic by Cantor and Conway, the ordinal numbers
themselves are the same and maintain the same order, i.e., say with conven-
tional designations αcan = {0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ...} = {0, 1, 2, ..., α′, ... | } = αcon and
αcan < αcan ⇔αcon < αcon.

Remark 17. Proposition 19 is true not only in the framework of the NBG
theory, which we implied when we talked about proper classes of interval num-
bers (+∞c−, +∞c+), but also in the theory of super-classes. In this case, every
number, say b , in the super-class (+∞λ, +∞λ
2 ) has (+∞c−, +∞c+) as its proper

this “jump“ (+∞λ, λ] of a sequence (α)0≤α<λ=ωµ, i.e., there is a “perpetuum
mobile“ increasing and decreasing sequence (bα)0≤α, bα ∈(+∞λ, +∞λ
2 ) such

2 ) are linear ordered
and disjoint. However, for the ordinal λ = ωµ+1, the “jump“ (+∞λ, λ] of a se-
quence (α)0≤α<λ=ωµ+1 is in the same sense incredibly larger than the previous
“jump“.
We note that a certain law of “jumps“ emerges. The first “jump“ (+∞ω, ω]
is repeated ω-times, i.e., (+∞2·ω, 2 · ω], ..., (+∞n·ω, n · ω], ... , 1 < n < ω, and
then a greater “jump“ (+∞ω2, ω2] occurs. Next, the second “jump“ is repeated
ω-times along with the ω-times of the first “jump“ , i.e., (+∞2·ω2, 2 · ω2], ...,
(+∞n·ω2, n · ω2], ... , 1 < n < ω, and a third “jump“ is made (+∞ω3, ω3] ,
greater than all the previous ones combined, and so on. In a word, there is
a kind of periodically increasing pulsating flow of “jumps“, shortly called here
“pulsars“.
89


## Page 90


There is a certain cyclicity or repetition of “jumps“ between the limit or-
dinals of the form ωµ, 0 < µ < Ω.
Additionally, all “jumps“ of the same
magnitude can be listed using ordinal numbers, as they are well-ordered.
Proposition 20. All “jumps“ (+∞λ, λ], for all limit ordinals ω ≤... <
λ < ωµ, for a fixed ordinal 0 < µ < Ω, are well-ordered and moreover, all equal
“jumps“ between them can be calculated by types of the well-ordered sets.
Proof. It is clear that all “jumps“ (+∞λ, λ], for all limit ordinals ω ≤... <
λ < ωµ, for a fixed ordinal 0 < µ < Ω, are well-ordered by the ends λ of intervals
(+∞λ, λ].
The second part of Proposition 20 can be proved by the transfinite induction.
We start with the beginning of induction λ = ω. There the only one “jumps“
(+∞ω]. The second “jumps“ (+∞2·ω, 2·]. The nth “jumps“ is (+∞n·ω, n · ω].
The ωth “jumps“ is (+∞ω2, ω2]. Moreover, we correspond to each “jumps“ is
(+∞α·ω, α · ω] an ordinal α · ω, 0 < α < ω, and for (+∞ω2, ω2] we correspond
an ordinal ω2. In total, we have ω “jumps“ “length“ (+∞ω, ω] and one “jump“
length“ (+∞ω2, ω2] or, what is the same ων, 2 > ν ≥0. Similar for λ = ωn,
i.e., n > ν ≥0 or what is the same, we have ω “jumps“ “length“ (+∞ω, ω] and
one “jump“ length“ (+∞ω2, ω2]. □

Remark 18. Note that the proof of Proposition 21 is not so simple and
short. The fact is that defining the functions ex for a finite Conway number x
that is not a real number and ln x when x is not a positive real number (note
also that always x is not a infinitesimal number) is not so simple and it will take
several pages to identify them (see [25], pp. 25-31).
Remark 19. Of course, there is a more general mapping ¯φ : C[i] →C[i]\{0}
of groups given by the following formula: ¯φ(x+iy) = ez = ex ·eiy = ex ·(cos y +
i sin y), where z = x+iy ∈C[i], x, y ∈No and cos y = cos y′′ and sin y = sin y′′,
y = y′ + y′′ (see paragraph: 2.2 above), but we will omit this extension, which
is the same as the previous one, except that this homomorphism ¯φ is not a
monomorphism because it is periodic along the imaginary axis, i.e., ez+i2π = ez.

And so on and so forth.
In conclusion, I would like to say two things. The form of Conway’s numbers
is the most successful form I know, i.e., x = {L | R}, [14], p. 4, allowing us
to see the unique feature of ordinal numbers λ = {L | }, [14], p. 26, i.e., when
R = ∅, with their pulsating periodic “jumps“, as well as the role of real numbers
x = {x −(1/n) | x −(1/n)}n>0, [14], p. 24, in defining Conway’s exponential
function, [14], p. 31, and the normal form of number x = P
β<α
ωyβ · rβ, [14], p.
33.
And of course Conway’s principle of induction: “Objects may be created
from earlier objects in any reasonably constructive fashion“, [14], p. 66, allows
us to see general laws not only for Cantor’s “consistent multiplicity“, allowed
by formal systems, but also for cases of undefinable multiplicity, such as eternal
sequences (α)α>0 that Cantor considered as “inconsistent multiplicity“.
